The Force Sequences Database

march to a million: in progress

Archive records: 24 / 1,000,000 target · 24 records (populated from the live archive index)

The force sequences database holds one ordered sequence of force applications per record: its steps in order, a verdict per step, and a verdict for the whole sequence, whether every step holds, holds only under a stated condition, or fails, runs away, or collapses. Each record states the physics basis in net force per step, equilibrium conditions, and thresholds where established, and carries a deterministic force-sequence demo that recomputes every step's verdict from fixed inputs. Distinction, stated plainly: the live network holds force fields and force-and-motion basics, and the sibling pair holds force combinations, but no database of ordered force sequences with a collapse-or-not verdict per step and per sequence; this pair is that missing verdict archive, one sequence per record, ordered simple to compounded to complex. No record here carries weapons design data of any kind, and no sequence is given any weapons application. Twenty-four full records, ordered simple to compounded to complex, each written in human-readable sentences and paragraphs, each carrying its ordered steps with the forces at each step, its sequence rule, its per-step and whole-sequence collapse verdicts, its physics basis, a worked example, a history, a science proof, a math proof, a formal statement, a scientific equation, a proof sketch, a runnable demo program whose output is reproduced on the page, and a named specialist AI. Every demo adds the member force vectors at each step, compares the net force with the force that step's motion requires, runs the whole sequence twice, and matches the two SHA-256 hashes; no demo is a laboratory measurement, and no record carries weapons design data.

Specialist AI: JAH Force Sequence Guide

JAH Force Sequence Guide answers questions about this database, explains any record in plain sentences, compares records side by side on the same fields, and helps a reader follow the ladder from simple to compounded to complex. It never presents a generated design as a public record, and it never presents a design as an established public fact.

Boundless generator

The generator is seeded and deterministic, throttled to at most 100 records per call, duplicate-checked by deterministic hash ids, and hard-stopped at 1,000,000,000 records. Every generated record is labeled src "generated". Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files. Open the page script to use window.ForceSequenceTools.

Validation engine

Each record is checked for a complete title, its own record kind, its own edition slug and id prefix, a full prose description, a subject, a problem of at least 400 characters, a method, ordered sequence steps with forces, magnitudes and directions at each step, a sequence rule, a collapse verdict, a physics basis, a worked example, a history, an evidence label with its reason, a verification path, a science proof, a math proof, a formal statement, a scientific equation, a proof sketch, a named source, and a demo whose stored output byte-matches a fresh run and whose verdict matches the stated collapse verdict.

Elevator Ride: Motor Pull, Cruise, Brake, Stop Rank 1 · simple

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: elevator acceleration and motor force balance are settled and measured on every elevator ride.

A 1000 kilogram elevator car riding upward: the motor pulls, the car cruises at steady speed, the motor brakes, and the car stops at the floor.

An elevator ride is four force balances in a row, and the ride only feels smooth because each one is right. A 1000 kilogram car weighs 9810 newtons. To accelerate upward the motor must pull with 11,010 newtons, so the net force is 1200 newtons up and the car gains speed. At cruising speed the motor pulls with exactly 9810 newtons, the net force is zero, and the car rises at a steady speed. To slow down the motor pulls with only 8610 newtons, the net force is 1200 newtons down, and the car loses speed. At the floor the motor pulls with 9810 newtons again, the net force is zero, and the car stands still. If any step's pull is wrong, the car jerks, overshoots, or stalls.

History

Elisha Otis demonstrated his safety brake at the New York Crystal Palace exhibition in 1853, cutting the rope and showing the car would not fall, and the electric traction elevator that followed turned the elevator into the standard way to move people up tall buildings. The four-phase ride, accelerate, cruise, brake, stop, is the control pattern every modern elevator controller still follows, with the motor pull adjusted continuously to hold the planned acceleration.

Method

Weigh the car and compute its weight from mass times gravity.; Set the acceleration and compute the motor pull needed for the first step.; Check each step's net force against the net force that step's motion requires.; Confirm the cruise step has zero net force at steady speed.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Accelerate up — forces:
    • Motor pull: 11010 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 9810 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 1200 N; actual net force Fx = -1.1279e-12 N, Fy = 1200 N; difference 1.1279e-12 N (tolerance 5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Cruise — forces:
    • Motor pull: 9810 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 9810 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.20138e-12 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.20138e-12 N (tolerance 5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Brake — forces:
    • Motor pull: 8610 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 9810 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = -1200 N; actual net force Fx = -1.27486e-12 N, Fy = -1200 N; difference 1.27486e-12 N (tolerance 5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Stop — forces:
    • Motor pull: 9810 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 9810 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.20138e-12 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.20138e-12 N (tolerance 5 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: accelerate, cruise, brake, stop. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

stable — Critical value: motor pull 11,010 newtons in the accelerate step. A motor that cannot supply it cannot start the car upward.

Physics basis

Step 1, accelerate up: motor pull 11,010 newtons up against gravity 9810 newtons down gives a net force of 1200 newtons up, which is the force the acceleration requires. Step 2, cruise: pull 9810 newtons against gravity 9810 newtons gives zero net force at steady speed. Step 3, brake: pull 8610 newtons against gravity 9810 newtons gives 1200 newtons down, which slows the car. Step 4, stop: pull 9810 newtons against gravity 9810 newtons gives zero net force and the car stands still.

Worked example

A 1000 kilogram elevator car leaves the ground floor. The motor pulls with 11,010 newtons, the car accelerates upward at 1.2 metres per second squared, and after a few seconds it is rising at its cruising speed. The motor pull drops to 9810 newtons, the car rises steadily, and the passengers feel their normal weight. Near the top floor the pull drops to 8610 newtons, the car slows, and at the floor the pull returns to 9810 newtons and the doors open with the car standing still.

Evidence

established — Established textbook physics: elevator acceleration and motor force balance are settled and measured on every elevator ride.

Verification path

Check by hand: 1000 times 9.81 is 9810, and 11,010 minus 9810 is 1200, matching the accelerate step, while 8610 minus 9810 is minus 1200, matching the brake step. The cruise and stop steps are exact balances, 9810 against 9810, so their net force is zero.

Generator

Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

Science proof

In plain words, the motor does not lift the car by brute force alone; it lifts it by pulling a little harder than gravity while the car speeds up, exactly as hard as gravity while it cruises, and a little less hard while it slows down. The difference between the pull and the weight is the net force, and the net force is what changes the speed. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each of the four steps in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic, step by step: Step 'Accelerate up': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.1279e-12 N and Fy = 1200 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 1200 N; the difference is 1.1279e-12 N against a tolerance of 5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Cruise': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.20138e-12 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.20138e-12 N against a tolerance of 5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Brake': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.27486e-12 N and Fy = -1200 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = -1200 N; the difference is 1.27486e-12 N against a tolerance of 5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Stop': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.20138e-12 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.20138e-12 N against a tolerance of 5 N, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

Motor pull = m * g + m * a; accelerate: 9810 + 1200 = 11,010 N; cruise: 9810 N; brake: 9810 - 1200 = 8610 N (stated, not machine-checked)

Scientific equation

In plain words, the motor does not lift the car by brute force alone; it lifts it by pulling a little harder than gravity while the car speeds up, exactly as hard as gravity while it cruises, and a little less hard while it slows down. The difference between the pull and the weight is the net force, and the net force is what changes the speed. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each of the four steps in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Proof sketch

Worked arithmetic, step by step: Step 'Accelerate up': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.1279e-12 N and Fy = 1200 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 1200 N; the difference is 1.1279e-12 N against a tolerance of 5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Cruise': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.20138e-12 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.20138e-12 N against a tolerance of 5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Brake': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.27486e-12 N and Fy = -1200 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = -1200 N; the difference is 1.27486e-12 N against a tolerance of 5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Stop': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.20138e-12 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.20138e-12 N against a tolerance of 5 N, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Elevator Ride: Motor Pull, Cruise, Brake, Stop'
steps = [('Accelerate up', [('Motor pull', 11010.0, 90), ('Gravity', 9810.0, 270)], [0.0, 1200.0], 5.0), ('Cruise', [('Motor pull', 9810.0, 90), ('Gravity', 9810.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 5.0), ('Brake', [('Motor pull', 8610.0, 90), ('Gravity', 9810.0, 270)], [0.0, -1200.0], 5.0), ('Stop', [('Motor pull', 9810.0, 90), ('Gravity', 9810.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 5.0)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Car Start, Cruise, and Braking Stop Rank 2 · simple

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: drive-minus-resistance acceleration and braking deceleration are settled and measured on every road.

A 1000 kilogram car on a flat road: the engine drives it away from rest, it cruises at steady speed, it brakes, and it stops.

A car journey is a sequence of horizontal force balances, and each step has its own net force. A 1000 kilogram car weighs 9810 newtons, held up by the same normal force from the road. To start, the engine drives forward with 3000 newtons against 200 newtons of rolling resistance, so the net force is 2800 newtons forward and the car accelerates. At cruising speed the drive is 200 newtons, exactly matching the resistance, so the net force is zero and the speed stays steady. To stop, the brakes supply 5000 newtons backward plus 200 newtons of resistance, so the net force is 5200 newtons backward and the car slows. At rest every horizontal force is zero.

History

The first automobiles of the 1880s and 1890s had to solve exactly this sequence: enough drive to overcome resistance and accelerate, a steady cruise where drive equals resistance, and brakes strong enough to stop the car in a reasonable distance. The drum brake, then the disc brake, and then anti-lock control each improved one step of the sequence, but the force accounting has not changed since.

Method

Weigh the car and compute the weight and the normal force.; Measure the drive force and the rolling resistance at each step.; Add the horizontal forces at each step to get the net force.; Compare each step's net force with the net force that step's motion requires.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Start — forces:
    • Drive force: 3000 N at 0 degrees
    • Rolling resistance: 200 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 9810 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 9810 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 2800 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = 2800 N, Fy = 2.44929e-14 N; difference 1.36446e-12 N (tolerance 5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Cruise — forces:
    • Drive force: 200 N at 0 degrees
    • Rolling resistance: 200 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 9810 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 9810 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.20138e-12 N, Fy = 2.44929e-14 N; difference 1.20163e-12 N (tolerance 5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Brake — forces:
    • Braking force: 5000 N at 180 degrees
    • Rolling resistance: 200 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 9810 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 9810 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = -5200 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -5200 N, Fy = 6.36816e-13 N; difference 1.11028e-12 N (tolerance 5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Stop — forces:
    • Normal force: 9810 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 9810 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.20138e-12 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.20138e-12 N (tolerance 5 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: start, cruise, brake, stop. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

stable — Critical value: braking force 5000 newtons in the brake step. Weaker brakes stretch the stopping distance; the sequence needs the full value.

Physics basis

Step 1, start: drive 3000 newtons forward against rolling resistance 200 newtons backward gives a net force of 2800 newtons forward, which accelerates the car. Step 2, cruise: drive 200 newtons against resistance 200 newtons gives zero net force at steady speed. Step 3, brake: braking force 5000 newtons plus resistance 200 newtons backward gives 5200 newtons backward, which slows the car. Step 4, stop: no horizontal forces act, so the net force is zero and the car stays still.

Worked example

A 1000 kilogram car pulls away from a traffic light. The engine drives with 3000 newtons, resistance takes 200, and the car accelerates at 2.8 metres per second squared. On the open road the driver eases off until the drive is 200 newtons, matching resistance, and the car cruises at a steady speed. At the next light the brakes supply 5000 newtons backward, the car slows at 5.2 metres per second squared, and it stops with the engine idling and no horizontal force acting.

Evidence

established — Established textbook physics: drive-minus-resistance acceleration and braking deceleration are settled and measured on every road.

Verification path

Check by hand: 3000 minus 200 is 2800, matching the start step, and 5000 plus 200 is 5200, matching the brake step. The cruise step is an exact balance, 200 against 200, and the stop step has no horizontal forces at all, so both have zero net force.

Science proof

In plain words, a car only cares about the difference between the forward push and the backward drag. While the push is bigger the car speeds up, while they are equal the car holds its speed, and while the backward force is bigger the car slows down. The demo proves the arithmetic by adding the horizontal forces at each of the four steps in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic, step by step: Step 'Start': adding its member vectors gives Fx = 2800 N and Fy = 2.44929e-14 N, against a required net force of Fx = 2800 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.36446e-12 N against a tolerance of 5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Cruise': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.20138e-12 N and Fy = 2.44929e-14 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.20163e-12 N against a tolerance of 5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Brake': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -5200 N and Fy = 6.36816e-13 N, against a required net force of Fx = -5200 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.11028e-12 N against a tolerance of 5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Stop': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.20138e-12 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.20138e-12 N against a tolerance of 5 N, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

F_net = drive - resistance; start: 3000 - 200 = 2800 N; cruise: 200 - 200 = 0 N; brake: -(5000 + 200) = -5200 N (stated, not machine-checked)

Scientific equation

In plain words, a car only cares about the difference between the forward push and the backward drag. While the push is bigger the car speeds up, while they are equal the car holds its speed, and while the backward force is bigger the car slows down. The demo proves the arithmetic by adding the horizontal forces at each of the four steps in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Car Start, Cruise, and Braking Stop'
steps = [('Start', [('Drive force', 3000.0, 0), ('Rolling resistance', 200.0, 180), ('Normal force', 9810.0, 90), ('Gravity', 9810.0, 270)], [2800.0, 0.0], 5.0), ('Cruise', [('Drive force', 200.0, 0), ('Rolling resistance', 200.0, 180), ('Normal force', 9810.0, 90), ('Gravity', 9810.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 5.0), ('Brake', [('Braking force', 5000.0, 180), ('Rolling resistance', 200.0, 180), ('Normal force', 9810.0, 90), ('Gravity', 9810.0, 270)], [-5200.0, 0.0], 5.0), ('Stop', [('Normal force', 9810.0, 90), ('Gravity', 9810.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 5.0)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")

    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))

Ball Thrown Upward: Rise, Apex, Fall, Bounce Rank 3 · simple

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: projectile motion under gravity and drag is settled, measured, and taught everywhere.

A 0.5 kilogram ball thrown straight up: the hand pushes it, it rises against gravity and drag, it pauses at the apex, and it falls back and bounces.

A thrown ball passes through four force regimes, and gravity is in charge of three of them. A 0.5 kilogram ball weighs 4.9 newtons. The hand pushes up with 20 newtons, so the net force is 15.1 newtons up and the ball leaves the hand fast. In the rise, gravity pulls down with 4.9 newtons and air drag adds 0.5 newtons down, so the net force is 5.4 newtons down and the ball slows. At the apex gravity alone acts, 4.9 newtons down, and the ball pauses for an instant before falling. On the ground the floor pushes up with 50 newtons against the 4.9 newton weight, so the net force is 45.1 newtons up and the ball rebounds.

History

Galileo showed that falling bodies accelerate uniformly under gravity regardless of their mass, and Newton gave the force account: the same gravity that pulls the ball down on the way up also pulls it down on the way down, so the rise and the fall are mirror images apart from air drag. The thrown ball has been the standard textbook example of changing net force ever since.

Method

Weigh the ball and compute its weight from mass times gravity.; Take the hand push and subtract the weight to get the launch net force.; Add gravity and drag during the rise and at the apex.; Take the floor's push at the bounce and subtract the weight.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Launch push — forces:
    • Hand push: 20 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 4.9 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 15.1 N; actual net force Fx = 3.24531e-16 N, Fy = 15.1 N; difference 3.24531e-16 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Rise — forces:
    • Gravity: 4.9 N at 270 degrees
    • Air drag: 0.5 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = -5.4 N; actual net force Fx = -9.91964e-16 N, Fy = -5.4 N; difference 9.91964e-16 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Apex — forces:
    • Gravity: 4.9 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = -4.9 N; actual net force Fx = -9.00115e-16 N, Fy = -4.9 N; difference 9.00115e-16 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Bounce — forces:
    • Ground normal force: 50 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 4.9 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 45.1 N; actual net force Fx = 2.1615e-15 N, Fy = 45.1 N; difference 2.1615e-15 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: launch push, rise, apex, bounce. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

stable — Critical value: hand push 20 newtons in the launch step. A weaker push gives a lower throw; the sequence needs the stated push for the stated flight.

Physics basis

Step 1, launch push: hand push 20 newtons up against gravity 4.9 newtons down gives a net force of 15.1 newtons up. Step 2, rise: gravity 4.9 newtons plus air drag 0.5 newtons downward gives 5.4 newtons down, which slows the ball. Step 3, apex: gravity 4.9 newtons down alone gives 4.9 newtons down at the instant of zero speed. Step 4, bounce: ground push 50 newtons up against gravity 4.9 newtons down gives 45.1 newtons up, which rebounds the ball.

Worked example

A child throws a 0.5 kilogram ball straight up with a 20 newton push. It leaves the hand accelerating upward, slows all the way to the top of its arc, hangs for an instant at the apex, and then falls back, speeding up all the way down. It hits the ground, the floor pushes back with 50 newtons, and the ball springs back up for a smaller second throw.

Evidence

established — Established textbook physics: projectile motion under gravity and drag is settled, measured, and taught everywhere.

Verification path

Check by hand: 0.5 times 9.81 is 4.9, 20 minus 4.9 is 15.1 for the launch step, and 4.9 plus 0.5 is 5.4 for the rise step. The bounce step is 50 minus 4.9, which is 45.1, matching the stated net force.

Science proof

In plain words, the hand only matters for a moment; after the ball leaves the hand, gravity takes over and never lets go. It slows the ball on the way up, stops it for an instant at the top, and speeds it up on the way down. The bounce is the floor pushing back harder than gravity pulls, which is what sends the ball up again. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Ball Thrown Upward: Rise, Apex, Fall, Bounce'
steps = [('Launch push', [('Hand push', 20.0, 90), ('Gravity', 4.9, 270)], [0.0, 15.1], 0.5), ('Rise', [('Gravity', 4.9, 270), ('Air drag', 0.5, 270)], [0.0, -5.4], 0.5), ('Apex', [('Gravity', 4.9, 270)], [0.0, -4.9], 0.5), ('Bounce', [('Ground normal force', 50.0, 90), ('Gravity', 4.9, 270)], [0.0, 45.1], 0.5)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Spring Compression, Release, Oscillation, Settle Rank 4 · simple

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: Hooke's law and mass-spring oscillation are settled and directly measurable.

A 5 kilogram mass on a spring of stiffness 100 newtons per metre: the hand compresses it, releases it, it oscillates, and it settles at equilibrium.

A mass on a spring runs through four force regimes, and the spring force changes at every one. A 5 kilogram mass weighs 49.05 newtons. Held compressed, the hand pushes down with 30 newtons, the spring pushes up with 79.05 newtons, and gravity pulls down with 49.05 newtons, so the net force is zero. On release the hand lets go, the spring's 79.05 newtons up beats gravity's 49.05 newtons down, and the net force is 30 newtons up, so the mass shoots upward. Passing equilibrium the spring force is 49.05 newtons, exactly matching gravity, so the net force is zero. At rest the same balance holds, and the mass settles.

History

Hooke stated the spring law, that extension is proportional to force, in 1678, and the mass-spring system became the standard model of oscillation. The compress-release-oscillate-settle sequence is the behaviour every spring is judged by, from a mattress to a car suspension, and the equilibrium stretch, weight divided by stiffness, is the first calculation taught with it.

Method

Weigh the mass and compute its weight from mass times gravity.; Take the spring stiffness and compute the spring force at the compressed stretch.; Add hand, spring, and gravity at the held step and check the sum is zero.; Remove the hand force for the release step and recompute the net force.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Compress — forces:
    • Hand push: 30 N at 270 degrees
    • Spring force: 79.05 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 49.05 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -9.68083e-15 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 9.68083e-15 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Release — forces:
    • Spring force: 79.05 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 49.05 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 30 N; actual net force Fx = -4.16992e-15 N, Fy = 30 N; difference 4.16992e-15 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Oscillation — forces:
    • Spring force: 49.05 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 49.05 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -6.00689e-15 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 6.00689e-15 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Settle — forces:
    • Spring force: 49.05 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 49.05 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -6.00689e-15 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 6.00689e-15 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: compress, release, oscillation, settle. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

stable — Critical value: spring force 79.05 newtons at the compressed stretch. The spring must stay inside its elastic range or the law stops applying.

Physics basis

Step 1, compress: spring force 79.05 newtons up against hand push 30 newtons down and gravity 49.05 newtons down gives zero net force. Step 2, release: spring force 79.05 newtons up against gravity 49.05 newtons down gives 30 newtons up, which launches the mass. Step 3, oscillation: spring force 49.05 newtons up against gravity 49.05 newtons down gives zero net force at the equilibrium point. Step 4, settle: the same balance holds at rest, so the mass stays put.

Worked example

A student presses a 5 kilogram mass down on a spring until the spring force is 79.05 newtons, holding it with a 30 newton push. Let go, and the mass leaps upward, overshoots the equilibrium point, swings back down, and oscillates smaller and smaller until it hangs still at the stretch of 0.4905 metres, where the spring force of 49.05 newtons exactly matches the weight.

Evidence

established — Established textbook physics: Hooke's law and mass-spring oscillation are settled and directly measurable.

Verification path

Check by hand: 5 times 9.81 is 49.05, and 79.05 minus 49.05 minus 30 is zero for the compress step, while 79.05 minus 49.05 is 30 for the release step. At equilibrium the spring force is 49.05 newtons, matching the weight exactly.

Release the mass and the extra spring force throws it upward; pass the balance point and gravity takes over. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. import json

title = 'Spring Compression, Release, Oscillation, Settle'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Crate Push: Break Static Friction, Slide, Stop Rank 5 · simple

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: static versus kinetic friction and the break-free force are settled and routinely measured.

A 10 kilogram crate on a floor: the push breaks static friction, the crate slides against kinetic friction, the push stops, and the crate comes to rest.

Pushing a crate is a sequence because friction changes its character halfway through. A 10 kilogram crate weighs 98.1 newtons. At first static friction grips with up to 49.05 newtons, from a coefficient of 0.5 times the normal force, and a 50 newton push beats it by 0.95 newtons, so the crate breaks free. Once sliding, kinetic friction is only 29.43 newtons, from a coefficient of 0.3, so the same 50 newton push leaves 20.57 newtons forward and the crate accelerates. Stop pushing and kinetic friction alone, 29.43 newtons backward, slows the crate to a stop. At rest the push is gone, friction is zero, and the crate stays put.

History

Static and kinetic friction were measured systematically by Amontons around 1699 and by Coulomb in the 1780s, who found that the force needed to start a body moving is larger than the force needed to keep it moving. The pushed crate, with its break-free moment, has been the standard demonstration of the two coefficients ever since.

Method

Weigh the crate and compute the normal force from its weight.; Compute the maximum static friction from the static coefficient.; Compare the push with the static limit to find the break-free step.; Compute kinetic friction from the kinetic coefficient for the sliding steps.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Break free — forces:
    • Applied push: 50 N at 0 degrees
    • Static friction: 49.05 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 98.1 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 98.1 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0.95 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = 0.95 N, Fy = 6.00689e-15 N; difference 1.0907e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Slide — forces:
    • Applied push: 50 N at 0 degrees
    • Kinetic friction: 29.43 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 98.1 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 98.1 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 20.57 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = 20.57 N, Fy = 3.60414e-15 N; difference 1.1251e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Stop pushing — forces:
    • Kinetic friction: 29.43 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 98.1 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 98.1 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = -29.43 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -29.43 N, Fy = 3.60414e-15 N; difference 1.1251e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: At rest — forces:
    • Normal force: 98.1 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 98.1 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.20138e-14 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.20138e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: break free, slide, stop pushing, at rest. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

stable — Critical value: static friction limit 49.05 newtons. A push below it never starts the crate, and the sequence never begins.

Physics basis

Step 1, break free: push 50 newtons forward against static friction 49.05 newtons backward gives a net force of 0.95 newtons forward, which starts the crate moving. Step 2, slide: push 50 newtons against kinetic friction 29.43 newtons gives 20.57 newtons forward, which accelerates the crate. Step 3, stop pushing: kinetic friction 29.43 newtons backward alone gives 29.43 newtons backward, which slows the crate. Step 4, at rest: no horizontal forces act, so the net force is zero and the crate stays put.

Worked example

A worker pushes a 10 kilogram crate with 50 newtons. At first nothing happens, then the crate jerks free as the push passes the 49.05 newton static limit. It slides more easily than expected because kinetic friction is only 29.43 newtons, so the crate accelerates at 2.06 metres per second squared. The worker stops pushing, friction alone slows the crate, and it comes to rest a few metres on.

Evidence

established — Established textbook physics: static versus kinetic friction and the break-free force are settled and routinely measured.

Verification path

Check by hand: 0.5 times 98.1 is 49.05, so 50 minus 49.05 is 0.95 for the break-free step, and 0.3 times 98.1 is 29.43, so 50 minus 29.43 is 20.57 for the slide step. The stopping step is friction alone, 29.43 newtons backward.

Generator

Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

Science proof

In plain words, friction is stronger before things move than while they move, so the hardest part of pushing a crate is the first centimetre. Once it slides, the same push has more force left over, which is why the crate suddenly speeds up. Stop pushing and the same friction that resisted the start now eats the motion until the crate stops. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic, step by step: Step 'Break free': adding its member vectors gives Fx = 0.95 N and Fy = 6.00689e-15 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0.95 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.0907e-14 N against a tolerance of 0.5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Slide': adding its member vectors gives Fx = 20.57 N and Fy = 3.60414e-15 N, against a required net force of Fx = 20.57 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.1251e-14 N against a tolerance of 0.5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Stop pushing': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -29.43 N and Fy = 3.60414e-15 N, against a required net force of Fx = -29.43 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.1251e-14 N against a tolerance of 0.5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'At rest': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.20138e-14 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.20138e-14 N against a tolerance of 0.5 N, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

Static limit = mu_s * N = 0.5 * 98.1 = 49.05 N; kinetic friction = mu_k * N = 0.3 * 98.1 = 29.43 N; slide net = 50 - 29.43 = 20.57 N (stated, not machine-checked)

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Crate Push: Break Static Friction, Slide, Stop'
steps = [('Break free', [('Applied push', 50.0, 0), ('Static friction', 49.05, 180), ('Normal force', 98.1, 90), ('Gravity', 98.1, 270)], [0.95, 0.0], 0.5), ('Slide', [('Applied push', 50.0, 0), ('Kinetic friction', 29.43, 180), ('Normal force', 98.1, 90), ('Gravity', 98.1, 270)], [20.57, 0.0], 0.5), ('Stop pushing', [('Kinetic friction', 29.43, 180), ('Normal force', 98.1, 90), ('Gravity', 98.1, 270)], [-29.43, 0.0], 0.5), ('At rest', [('Normal force', 98.1, 90), ('Gravity', 98.1, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.5)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Pendulum Release, Swing, Damp, Rest Rank 6 · simple

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: pendulum dynamics and centripetal force are settled, measured, and taught everywhere.

A 10 kilogram bob on a 1 metre string: released from 30 degrees, it swings through the bottom, rises to the other side, and settles at rest.

A pendulum swing is a sequence because the forces change direction at every point of the arc. A 10 kilogram bob weighs 98.1 newtons. Released at 30 degrees from vertical, the string tension is 84.96 newtons along the string, and with gravity the net force is 49.05 newtons along the swing, which is mass times gravity times the sine of 30 degrees. At the bottom the tension is 117.72 newtons up against gravity 98.1 newtons down, so the net force is 19.62 newtons up, exactly the centripetal force the curved path needs at 1.4 metres per second. On the other side the same 49.05 newton net force acts the other way, and at rest the tension is 98.1 newtons, matching the weight.

History

Galileo timed pendulum swings with his pulse and found the period nearly independent of amplitude for small swings; Huygens built the first pendulum clock in 1656 and worked out the circular-motion force. The pendulum became the standard demonstration that a net force can be present and correct, because it is the force that bends the path.

Method

Measure the mass, the string length, and the release angle.; Resolve the tension along the string at the release angle.; Add tension and gravity at each step to get the net force.; Compare the bottom-step net force with the centripetal force the swing requires.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Release — forces:
    • Tension: 84.96 N at 60 degrees
    • Gravity: 98.1 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 42.48 N, Fy = -24.52 N; actual net force Fx = 42.48 N, Fy = -24.5225 N; difference 0.00248169 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Bottom of swing — forces:
    • Tension: 117.72 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 98.1 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 19.62 N; actual net force Fx = -1.08124e-14 N, Fy = 19.62 N; difference 1.13811e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Rise — forces:
    • Tension: 84.96 N at 120 degrees
    • Gravity: 98.1 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = -42.48 N, Fy = -24.52 N; actual net force Fx = -42.48 N, Fy = -24.5225 N; difference 0.00248169 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Rest — forces:
    • Tension: 98.1 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 98.1 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.20138e-14 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.20138e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: release, bottom of swing, rise, rest. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

stable — Critical value: string tension 117.72 newtons at the bottom of the swing. A string rated below the needed tension snaps at the fastest point.

Physics basis

Step 1, release: tension 84.96 newtons along the string at 60 degrees against gravity 98.1 newtons down gives a net force of 49.05 newtons along the swing. Step 2, bottom of swing: tension 117.72 newtons up against gravity 98.1 newtons down gives 19.62 newtons up, the centripetal force the arc requires. Step 3, rise: tension 84.96 newtons at 120 degrees against gravity gives 49.05 newtons along the swing the other way. Step 4, rest: tension 98.1 newtons up against gravity 98.1 newtons down gives zero net force.

Worked example

A 10 kilogram bob on a 1 metre string is pulled aside to 30 degrees and let go. It sweeps down, moving fastest at the bottom where the string pulls with 117.72 newtons, climbs the other side to nearly the same height, and swings back. Each swing is a little smaller than the last as air drag and the pivot eat the energy, until the bob hangs still at the bottom with the tension equal to its weight.

Evidence

established — Established textbook physics: pendulum dynamics and centripetal force are settled, measured, and taught everywhere.

Verification path

Check by hand: 10 times 9.81 times sin 30 degrees is 49.05, matching the release step, and 10 times 1.4 squared over 1 metre is 19.62, matching the bottom step, while 98.1 plus 19.62 is 117.72, matching the stated tension.

Generator

Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

Science proof

In plain words, a body moving in a curve needs a force pulling it toward the centre of the curve, or it would go straight by inertia. At the sides of the swing gravity has a component along the arc that speeds the bob up or slows it down; at the bottom the string must pull harder than gravity by exactly the amount the curve demands. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic, step by step: Step 'Release': adding its member vectors gives Fx = 42.48 N and Fy = -24.5225 N, against a required net force of Fx = 42.48 N and Fy = -24.52 N; the difference is 0.00248169 N against a tolerance of 0.5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Bottom of swing': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.08124e-14 N and Fy = 19.62 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 19.62 N; the difference is 1.13811e-14 N against a tolerance of 0.5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Rise': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -42.48 N and Fy = -24.5225 N, against a required net force of Fx = -42.48 N and Fy = -24.52 N; the difference is 0.00248169 N against a tolerance of 0.5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Rest': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.20138e-14 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.20138e-14 N against a tolerance of 0.5 N, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Formal statement

Release net = m * g * sin(30) = 49.05 N; bottom tension = m * g + m * v^2 / L = 98.1 + 19.62 = 117.72 N (stated, not machine-checked)

The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Pendulum Release, Swing, Damp, Rest'
steps = [('Release', [('Tension', 84.96, 60), ('Gravity', 98.1, 270)], [42.48, -24.52], 0.5), ('Bottom of swing', [('Tension', 117.72, 90), ('Gravity', 98.1, 270)], [0.0, 19.62], 0.5), ('Rise', [('Tension', 84.96, 120), ('Gravity', 98.1, 270)], [-42.48, -24.52], 0.5), ('Rest', [('Tension', 98.1, 90), ('Gravity', 98.1, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.5)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Rowing Stroke: Catch, Drive, Release, Recovery Rank 7 · simple

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: oar force minus water drag is settled, and rowing strokes are measured on every river.

A 100 kilogram rowing boat: the oars catch the water, drive the boat forward, release, and recover while the boat glides.

A rowing stroke is a sequence because the oar force appears and disappears while drag never leaves. A 100 kilogram boat weighs 981 newtons, held up by the same buoyancy from the water. At the catch the oars pull with 400 newtons against 100 newtons of water drag, so the net force is 300 newtons forward and the boat surges. In the drive the oars pull with 500 newtons against 150 newtons of drag, so the net force is 350 newtons forward. At the release the oars leave the water and drag alone, 150 newtons backward, slows the boat. In the recovery the rower slides forward while the boat glides against 50 newtons of drag, losing speed slowly until the next catch.

History

Rowing has been a working transport and a racing sport for millennia, from the galleys of the ancient Mediterranean to the Oxford and Cambridge Boat Race, first run in 1829. The stroke cycle, catch, drive, release, recovery, is the same in a racing shell and a fishing dinghy, and coaches still teach it as four phases with different forces in each.

Method

Weigh the boat and compute the weight and the buoyancy.; Measure the oar force and the water drag at each step.; Add the horizontal forces at each step to get the net force.; Compare each step's net force with the net force that step's motion requires.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Catch — forces:
    • Oar force: 400 N at 0 degrees
    • Water drag: 100 N at 180 degrees
    • Buoyancy: 981 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 981 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 300 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = 300 N, Fy = 1.22465e-14 N; difference 1.14345e-13 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Drive — forces:
    • Oar force: 500 N at 0 degrees
    • Water drag: 150 N at 180 degrees
    • Buoyancy: 981 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 981 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 350 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = 350 N, Fy = 1.83697e-14 N; difference 1.15161e-13 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Release — forces:
    • Water drag: 150 N at 180 degrees
    • Buoyancy: 981 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 981 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = -150 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -150 N, Fy = 1.83697e-14 N; difference 1.15161e-13 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Recovery — forces:
    • Water drag: 50 N at 180 degrees
    • Buoyancy: 981 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 981 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = -50 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -50 N, Fy = 6.12323e-15 N; difference 1.20947e-13 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: catch, drive, release, recovery. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

stable — Critical value: oar force 500 newtons in the drive step. A weaker drive gains less speed than the glide loses, and the boat never gets up to speed.

Physics basis

Step 1, catch: oar force 400 newtons forward against water drag 100 newtons backward gives a net force of 300 newtons forward. Step 2, drive: oar force 500 newtons against drag 150 newtons gives 350 newtons forward. Step 3, release: drag 150 newtons backward alone gives 150 newtons backward, which slows the boat. Step 4, recovery: drag 50 newtons backward alone gives 50 newtons backward while the boat glides.

Worked example

A rower in a single scull takes a stroke. The blades catch, the legs drive, and the boat surges forward as the oars pull with 500 newtons against 150 newtons of drag. The blades lift out, the boat glides and slows gently, and the rower slides forward on the seat for the next catch. Over a full race the boat never stops moving, it only speeds up and slows down in rhythm with the strokes.

Evidence

established — Established textbook physics: oar force minus water drag is settled, and rowing strokes are measured on every river.

Verification path

Check by hand: 400 minus 100 is 300 for the catch step, and 500 minus 150 is 350 for the drive step. The release and recovery steps are drag alone, 150 and 50 newtons backward, and the vertical forces cancel at every step, 981 against 981.

Science proof

In plain words, a rowing boat only moves when the oars pull harder than the water drags, and the moment the oars leave the water the drag starts winning and the boat slows. The stroke works because the drive is strong and the recovery is gentle, so the boat gains more speed in the drive than it loses in the glide. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

title = 'Rowing Stroke: Catch, Drive, Release, Recovery'

unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

    return results, overall

digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()

    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")

Seesaw Sequence: Load, Balance, Shift, Rebalance Rank 8 · simple

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: lever balance and the pivot force matching the total load are settled and directly measurable.

A playground seesaw: two children load it, it balances, one child shifts, and the pivot force is adjusted by rebalancing the load.

A seesaw is a sequence of vertical balances, and the pivot force must track the total weight at every step. At loading, a 300 newton child on the left and a 200 newton child on the right weigh 500 newtons together, so the pivot pushes up with 500 newtons and the net force is zero. When the right child moves to match at 300 newtons, the total is 600 newtons and the pivot pushes with 600 newtons. When the right child is joined by a friend at 400 newtons, the total is 700 newtons and the pivot pushes with 700 newtons. Rebalanced at 350 newtons a side, the total is still 700 newtons and the pivot still pushes with 700 newtons, so the seesaw sits level.

History

The seesaw is one of the oldest playground machines, a lever with the pivot in the middle, and it has been used to teach balance since antiquity: Archimedes stated the lever principle in the third century BCE, that equal weights at equal distances balance. The playground version adds children who move, so the balance has to be re-found at every step.

Method

Weigh each child and add the weights to get the total load.; Set the pivot force equal to the total load at each step.; Add the downward weights and the upward pivot force at each step.; Check that the sum is zero, which is the balance condition.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Load — forces:
    • Left child: 300 N at 270 degrees
    • Right child: 200 N at 270 degrees
    • Pivot force: 500 N at 90 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -6.12323e-14 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 6.12323e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Balance — forces:
    • Left child: 300 N at 270 degrees
    • Right child: 300 N at 270 degrees
    • Pivot force: 600 N at 90 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -7.34788e-14 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 7.34788e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Shift — forces:
    • Left child: 300 N at 270 degrees
    • Right child: 400 N at 270 degrees
    • Pivot force: 700 N at 90 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -8.57253e-14 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 8.57253e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Rebalance — forces:
    • Left child: 350 N at 270 degrees
    • Right child: 350 N at 270 degrees
    • Pivot force: 700 N at 90 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -8.57253e-14 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 8.57253e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: load, balance, shift, rebalance. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

stable — Critical value: pivot force 700 newtons in the shift and rebalance steps. A pivot rated below the total load cannot hold the seesaw up.

Physics basis

Step 1, load: left child 300 newtons down and right child 200 newtons down against pivot force 500 newtons up gives zero net force. Step 2, balance: 300 and 300 newtons down against pivot force 600 newtons up gives zero net force. Step 3, shift: 300 and 400 newtons down against pivot force 700 newtons up gives zero net force. Step 4, rebalance: 350 and 350 newtons down against pivot force 700 newtons up gives zero net force.

Worked example

Two children climb onto a seesaw. The heavier child sits on the left and the lighter on the right, and the pivot holds 500 newtons while the seesaw tips gently to the heavy side. The lighter child slides outward until the weights match, the pivot holds 600 newtons, and the seesaw levels. A third child joins the right side, the pivot takes 700 newtons, and the children shuffle until both sides carry 350 newtons and the board is level again.

Evidence

established — Established textbook physics: lever balance and the pivot force matching the total load are settled and directly measurable.

Verification path

Check by hand: 300 plus 200 is 500, 300 plus 300 is 600, 300 plus 400 is 700, and 350 plus 350 is 700, and in each step the pivot force equals the total, so every net force is zero.

Science proof

In plain words, the pivot does not decide the balance, the children do; the pivot only has to push up exactly as hard as all the children together weigh down, or the whole seesaw would fall. When a child moves, the total changes, and the pivot force has to change with it. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Seesaw Sequence: Load, Balance, Shift, Rebalance'
steps = [('Load', [('Left child', 300.0, 270), ('Right child', 200.0, 270), ('Pivot force', 500.0, 90)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.5), ('Balance', [('Left child', 300.0, 270), ('Right child', 300.0, 270), ('Pivot force', 600.0, 90)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.5), ('Shift', [('Left child', 300.0, 270), ('Right child', 400.0, 270), ('Pivot force', 700.0, 90)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.5), ('Rebalance', [('Left child', 350.0, 270), ('Right child', 350.0, 270), ('Pivot force', 700.0, 90)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.5)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Model Rocket: Thrust, Coast, Apogee, Parachute Descent, Touchdown Rank 9 · compounded

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: model rocket thrust, coast, and parachute descent are settled and flown by hobbyists everywhere.

A 1 kilogram model rocket: the motor burns, the rocket coasts to its highest point, the parachute opens, and it lands gently.

A model rocket flight is five force regimes in a row, and the parachute step is the one that decides whether the flight ends well. A 1 kilogram rocket weighs 9.81 newtons. Under thrust the motor pushes up with 30 newtons against gravity and 0.19 newtons of drag, so the net force is 20 newtons up and the rocket accelerates skyward. Coasting, gravity and 1 newton of drag pull down with 10.81 newtons, so the rocket slows. At apogee gravity alone pulls down with 9.81 newtons at the instant of zero speed. Under the parachute, drag pushes up with 9.81 newtons against the weight, so the net force is zero and the rocket falls at a steady, survivable speed. At touchdown the ground pushes up with 9.81 newtons and the rocket stands still.

History

Model rocketry began in the late 1950s when Orville Carlisle and G. Harry Stine developed small solid-fuel motors and recovery parachutes for hobbyists, and it became a standard school science activity. The flight profile, thrust, coast, apogee, parachute descent, touchdown, is the same sequence, at a smaller scale, as a full-size sounding rocket.

Method

Weigh the rocket and compute its weight from mass times gravity.; Take the motor thrust and subtract gravity and drag for the thrust step.; Add gravity and drag for the coast and apogee steps.; Set the parachute drag equal to the weight for the descent step.; Run the demo, which checks all five steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

    • Air drag: 0.19 N at 270 degrees

    • Gravity: 9.81 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = -9.81 N; actual net force Fx = -1.80207e-15 N, Fy = -9.81 N; difference 1.80207e-15 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Parachute descent — forces:
    • Parachute drag: 9.81 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 9.81 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.20138e-15 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.20138e-15 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  5. Step 5: Touchdown — forces:
    • Ground normal force: 9.81 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 9.81 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.20138e-15 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.20138e-15 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: thrust, coast, apogee, parachute descent, touchdown. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: the parachute must open at apogee. If it fails, the descent step has drag far below the weight and the rocket falls at speed.

Physics basis

Step 1, thrust: motor thrust 30 newtons up against gravity 9.81 newtons and drag 0.19 newtons down gives a net force of 20 newtons up. Step 2, coast: gravity 9.81 newtons plus drag 1 newton down gives 10.81 newtons down, which slows the rocket. Step 3, apogee: gravity 9.81 newtons down alone gives 9.81 newtons down at zero speed. Step 4, parachute descent: drag 9.81 newtons up against gravity 9.81 newtons down gives zero net force. Step 5, touchdown: ground push 9.81 newtons up against gravity 9.81 newtons down gives zero net force.

Worked example

A student launches a 1 kilogram model rocket from a school field. The motor burns for a second, the rocket streaks upward, and then it coasts silently to its highest point, where a small charge pops the parachute out. The rocket drifts down under the canopy at a few metres per second and touches down softly in the grass, ready to fly again.

Evidence

established — Established textbook physics: model rocket thrust, coast, and parachute descent are settled and flown by hobbyists everywhere.

Verification path

Check by hand: 30 minus 9.81 minus 0.19 is 20 for the thrust step, and 9.81 plus 1 is 10.81 for the coast step. The parachute step is an exact balance, 9.81 against 9.81, so its net force is zero.

Generator

Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

Science proof

In plain words, the motor only fires for a moment, and the rocket spends most of its flight coasting on the speed the motor gave it, slowing all the way up. The parachute works by making drag as big as the weight, so the rocket stops speeding up and falls gently instead of crashing. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Model Rocket: Thrust, Coast, Apogee, Parachute Descent, Touchdown'
steps = [('Thrust', [('Thrust', 30.0, 90), ('Gravity', 9.81, 270), ('Air drag', 0.19, 270)], [0.0, 20.0], 0.5), ('Coast', [('Gravity', 9.81, 270), ('Air drag', 1.0, 270)], [0.0, -10.81], 0.5), ('Apogee', [('Gravity', 9.81, 270)], [0.0, -9.81], 0.5), ('Parachute descent', [('Parachute drag', 9.81, 90), ('Gravity', 9.81, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.5), ('Touchdown', [('Ground normal force', 9.81, 90), ('Gravity', 9.81, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.5)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")
