Aircraft Takeoff, Climb, Cruise, Descent, Landing Rank 10 · compounded

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: the four forces of flight and the flight profile are settled and flown by airliners every day.

A 100,000 newton aircraft: it rolls down the runway, climbs, cruises, descends, and flares for landing, with lift, thrust, drag, and weight at every step.

An aircraft flight is a sequence of four-force balances, and lift is the force that changes most from step to step. The aircraft weighs 100,000 newtons. On the takeoff roll, thrust of 30,000 newtons beats drag of 5000 newtons while lift of 60,000 newtons is still short of the weight, so the net force is 25,000 newtons forward and 40,000 newtons down and the aircraft stays on the runway, accelerating. In the climb, lift of 110,000 newtons beats the weight and the net force is 15,000 newtons forward and 10,000 newtons up. In cruise, thrust matches drag at 15,000 newtons and lift matches weight at 100,000 newtons, so the net force is zero. In descent the net force is 4000 newtons backward and 5000 newtons down, and in the landing flare it is 3000 newtons backward with lift matching weight.

History

The Wright brothers flew the first powered aircraft in 1903, and the four forces of flight, lift, weight, thrust, and drag, have been the standard account ever since. The takeoff-climb-cruise-descent-landing sequence is the profile every pilot is trained to fly, and every airliner follows it on every flight, with the forces rebalanced at each step.

Method

Take the aircraft weight and the four forces at each step.; Add thrust and drag horizontally and lift and weight vertically at each step.; Compare each step's net force with the net force that step's motion requires.; Confirm the cruise step has zero net force in both directions.; Run the demo, which checks all five steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Takeoff roll — forces:
    • Thrust: 30000 N at 0 degrees
    • Drag: 5000 N at 180 degrees
    • Lift: 60000 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 100000 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 25000 N, Fy = -40000 N; actual net force Fx = 25000 N, Fy = -40000 N; difference 1.45519e-11 N (tolerance 5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Climb — forces:
    • Thrust: 25000 N at 0 degrees
    • Drag: 10000 N at 180 degrees
    • Lift: 110000 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 100000 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 15000 N, Fy = 10000 N; actual net force Fx = 15000 N, Fy = 10000 N; difference 1.10645e-11 N (tolerance 5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Cruise — forces:
    • Thrust: 15000 N at 0 degrees
    • Drag: 15000 N at 180 degrees
    • Lift: 100000 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 100000 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.22465e-11 N, Fy = 1.83697e-12 N; difference 1.23835e-11 N (tolerance 5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Descent — forces:
    • Thrust: 8000 N at 0 degrees
    • Drag: 12000 N at 180 degrees
    • Lift: 95000 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 100000 N at 270 degrees

    • Thrust: 5000 N at 0 degrees

    • Lift: 100000 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 100000 N at 270 degrees

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: takeoff roll, climb, cruise, descent, landing flare. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: lift must reach the weight before rotation, and thrust must match drag in cruise. Either shortfall breaks the sequence.

Physics basis

Step 1, takeoff roll: thrust 30,000 newtons against drag 5000 newtons gives 25,000 newtons forward, and lift 60,000 newtons against weight 100,000 newtons gives 40,000 newtons down, so the aircraft stays on the runway while accelerating. Step 2, climb: thrust 25,000 against drag 10,000 gives 15,000 newtons forward, and lift 110,000 against weight 100,000 gives 10,000 newtons up. Step 3, cruise: thrust 15,000 against drag 15,000 and lift 100,000 against weight 100,000 give zero net force. Step 4, descent: thrust 8000 against drag 12,000 gives 4000 newtons backward, and lift 95,000 against weight 100,000 gives 5000 newtons down. Step 5, landing flare: thrust 5000 against drag 8000 gives 3000 newtons backward, and lift 100,000 against weight 100,000 gives zero vertical net force.

Worked example

An airliner accelerates down the runway, its nose rises as lift grows past the weight, and it climbs away from the ground. At cruising altitude the engines settle to the thrust that exactly matches drag, and the wing lifts exactly the weight, so the aircraft flies level for hours. On approach the thrust drops, the aircraft descends, and in the flare the lift matches the weight again just as the wheels touch the runway.

Evidence

established — Established textbook physics: the four forces of flight and the flight profile are settled and flown by airliners every day.

Verification path

Check by hand: 30,000 minus 5000 is 25,000 and 60,000 minus 100,000 is minus 40,000 for the takeoff step, while 25,000 minus 10,000 is 15,000 and 110,000 minus 100,000 is 10,000 for the climb step. The cruise step is an exact balance in both directions.

Generator

Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

Science proof

In plain words, an aircraft only flies when its wing lifts as hard as gravity pulls, and it only moves forward when its engines push as hard as the air drags back. On the runway the wing is not lifting enough yet, so the aircraft stays down while it speeds up; in the air the balance is struck, and the aircraft cruises. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 5 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Math proof

Step 'Climb': adding its member vectors gives Fx = 15000 N and Fy = 10000 N, against a required net force of Fx = 15000 N and Fy = 10000 N; the difference is 1.10645e-11 N against a tolerance of 5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Cruise': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.22465e-11 N and Fy = 1.83697e-12 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.23835e-11 N against a tolerance of 5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Descent': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -4000 N and Fy = -5000 N, against a required net force of Fx = -4000 N and Fy = -5000 N; the difference is 1.28622e-11 N against a tolerance of 5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Landing flare': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -3000 N and Fy = 9.79717e-13 N, against a required net force of Fx = -3000 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.23172e-11 N against a tolerance of 5 N, so the step HOLDS. Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Aircraft Takeoff, Climb, Cruise, Descent, Landing'
steps = [('Takeoff roll', [('Thrust', 30000.0, 0), ('Drag', 5000.0, 180), ('Lift', 60000.0, 90), ('Gravity', 100000.0, 270)], [25000.0, -40000.0], 5.0), ('Climb', [('Thrust', 25000.0, 0), ('Drag', 10000.0, 180), ('Lift', 110000.0, 90), ('Gravity', 100000.0, 270)], [15000.0, 10000.0], 5.0), ('Cruise', [('Thrust', 15000.0, 0), ('Drag', 15000.0, 180), ('Lift', 100000.0, 90), ('Gravity', 100000.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 5.0), ('Descent', [('Thrust', 8000.0, 0), ('Drag', 12000.0, 180), ('Lift', 95000.0, 90), ('Gravity', 100000.0, 270)], [-4000.0, -5000.0], 5.0), ('Landing flare', [('Thrust', 5000.0, 0), ('Drag', 8000.0, 180), ('Lift', 100000.0, 90), ('Gravity', 100000.0, 270)], [-3000.0, 0.0], 5.0)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Satellite Launch: Thrust, Gravity Turn, Circularization, Orbit Rank 11 · compounded

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: launch profiles and orbital mechanics are settled, and satellites fly this sequence every day.

A 1000 kilogram satellite launch: vertical thrust, a gravity turn that bends the path sideways, a circularization burn, and orbit.

Getting to orbit is a sequence because the thrust direction has to change while gravity weakens with height. A 1000 kilogram satellite weighs 9810 newtons on the ground. In the vertical thrust step the engine pushes up with 15,000 newtons, so the net force is 5190 newtons up. In the gravity turn the engine pushes 12,000 newtons at 45 degrees while gravity pulls down with 9000 newtons, so the net force is 8485.28 newtons forward and 514.72 newtons down, bending the path sideways. The circularization burn adds 100 newtons forward while gravity pulls down with 9230 newtons at orbital height. In orbit, gravity of 9230 newtons toward the Earth plus a whisper of drag, 0.01 newtons, is exactly the centripetal force the circular path requires.

History

Newton showed in the Principia that a cannonball fired fast enough horizontally would fall around the Earth instead of into it, and the first artificial satellite, Sputnik 1, proved the calculation in 1957. The launch profile, vertical thrust, gravity turn, circularization burn, orbit, is the standard route to low Earth orbit, and every satellite operator still flies by that balance.

Method

Take the satellite mass and the thrust at each step.; Resolve the gravity-turn thrust into forward and vertical parts.; Add thrust and gravity at each step to get the net force.; Compare the orbit step's net force with the centripetal force the orbit requires.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Thrust — forces:
    • Thrust: 15000 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 9810 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 5190 N; actual net force Fx = -8.83583e-13 N, Fy = 5190 N; difference 8.83583e-13 N (tolerance 5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Gravity turn — forces:
    • Thrust: 12000 N at 45 degrees
    • Gravity: 9000 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 8485.28 N, Fy = -514.72 N; actual net force Fx = 8485.28 N, Fy = -514.719 N; difference 0.00194347 N (tolerance 1 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Circularization — forces:
    • Engine thrust: 100 N at 0 degrees
    • Gravity: 9230 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 100 N, Fy = -9230 N; actual net force Fx = 100 N, Fy = -9230 N; difference 1.69109e-12 N (tolerance 5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Orbit — forces:
    • Gravity: 9230 N at 270 degrees
    • Atmospheric drag: 0.01 N at 180 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = -9230 N; actual net force Fx = -0.01 N, Fy = -9230 N; difference 0.01 N (tolerance 5 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: thrust, gravity turn, circularization, orbit. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical value: circular-orbit speed 7.79 kilometres per second at 400 kilometres altitude. Slower means orbital decay; faster means a higher orbit.

Physics basis

Step 1, thrust: engine thrust 15,000 newtons up against gravity 9810 newtons down gives a net force of 5190 newtons up. Step 2, gravity turn: thrust 12,000 newtons at 45 degrees against gravity 9000 newtons down gives a net force of 8485.28 newtons forward and 514.72 newtons down. Step 3, circularization: engine thrust 100 newtons forward against gravity 9230 newtons down gives a net force of 100 newtons forward and 9230 newtons down. Step 4, orbit: gravity 9230 newtons toward the Earth plus drag 0.01 newtons against the motion gives the centripetal force the circular path requires, within tolerance.

Worked example

A rocket lifts a 1000 kilogram satellite straight up from the pad, then leans over into a gravity turn, its thrust bending the path sideways while gravity keeps pulling down. At orbital height a final burn circularizes the path, the engine cuts out, and the satellite circles the Earth every ninety minutes, falling around the planet and missing the ground, with only a whisper of air drag slowly decaying its orbit.

established — Established textbook physics: launch profiles and orbital mechanics are settled, and satellites fly this sequence every day.

Bungee Jump: Free Fall, Cord Stretch, Rebound, Settle Rank 12 · compounded

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: bungee cord stretch and rebound follow Hooke's law and are measured on every jump.

An 80 kilogram bungee jumper: free fall, the cord stretching to catch the fall, the rebound upward, and settling to a hang.

A bungee jump is a sequence because the cord force starts at zero, grows past the weight, and then settles to exactly the weight. An 80 kilogram jumper weighs 784.8 newtons. In free fall gravity alone pulls down with 784.8 newtons and the jumper accelerates. As the cord stretches it pulls up with 1200 newtons against the weight, so the net force is 415.2 newtons up and the fall slows, then reverses. On the rebound the cord pulls with 900 newtons, so the net force is 115.2 newtons up and the jumper rises again. After a few smaller bounces the cord pulls with 784.8 newtons, exactly matching the weight, so the net force is zero and the jumper hangs still.

History

Bungee jumping as a sport began with the Oxford University Dangerous Sports Club jump from the Clifton Suspension Bridge in 1979, inspired by the land-diving ritual of Pentecost Island in Vanuatu. The physics is the same as any spring: the cord stretches until its force matches the weight, and the jumper oscillates around that balance point until damping settles the motion.

Method

Weigh the jumper and compute the weight from mass times gravity.; Take the cord force at each step and subtract the weight.; Check that the free-fall step has gravity alone as its net force.; Confirm the settle step has the cord force exactly matching the weight.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Free fall — forces:
    • Gravity: 784.8 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = -784.8 N; actual net force Fx = -1.44165e-13 N, Fy = -784.8 N; difference 1.44165e-13 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Cord stretch — forces:
    • Cord force: 1200 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 784.8 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 415.2 N; actual net force Fx = -7.06866e-14 N, Fy = 415.2 N; difference 9.07071e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Rebound — forces:
    • Cord force: 900 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 784.8 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 115.2 N; actual net force Fx = -8.90563e-14 N, Fy = 115.2 N; difference 9.87348e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Settle — forces:
    • Cord force: 784.8 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 784.8 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -9.61103e-14 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 9.61103e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: free fall, cord stretch, rebound, settle. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: the cord must stretch enough to supply more than the weight before the jumper reaches the ground. Too short a cord, or too weak, breaks the sequence.

Physics basis

Step 1, free fall: gravity 784.8 newtons down alone gives a net force of 784.8 newtons down. Step 2, cord stretch: cord force 1200 newtons up against gravity 784.8 newtons down gives 415.2 newtons up, which slows and reverses the fall. Step 3, rebound: cord force 900 newtons up against gravity 784.8 newtons down gives 115.2 newtons up, which lifts the jumper again. Step 4, settle: cord force 784.8 newtons up against gravity 784.8 newtons down gives zero net force and the jumper hangs still.

Worked example

An 80 kilogram jumper steps off a bridge platform and falls freely, speeding up under gravity alone. The cord goes taut, stretches, and pulls up harder than the weight, so the fall slows, stops, and turns into a rebound upward. The jumper bounces a few times, each bounce smaller than the last, and finally hangs still beneath the bridge with the cord holding exactly the jumper's weight.

Evidence

established — Established textbook physics: bungee cord stretch and rebound follow Hooke's law and are measured on every jump.

Verification path

Check by hand: 80 times 9.81 is 784.8, 1200 minus 784.8 is 415.2 for the cord-stretch step, and 900 minus 784.8 is 115.2 for the rebound step. The settle step is an exact balance, 784.8 against 784.8, so its net force is zero.

Science proof

In plain words, the cord is a spring: it does nothing until it is stretched, then it pulls harder the further it stretches, until it is pulling harder than gravity and the fall turns around. The jumper bounces because the cord keeps overshooting the balance point, and settles because each bounce loses a little energy. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Bungee Jump: Free Fall, Cord Stretch, Rebound, Settle'
steps = [('Free fall', [('Gravity', 784.8, 270)], [0.0, -784.8], 0.5), ('Cord stretch', [('Cord force', 1200.0, 90), ('Gravity', 784.8, 270)], [0.0, 415.2], 0.5), ('Rebound', [('Cord force', 900.0, 90), ('Gravity', 784.8, 270)], [0.0, 115.2], 0.5), ('Settle', [('Cord force', 784.8, 90), ('Gravity', 784.8, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.5)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Roller Coaster: Lift Hill, Drop, Loop, Brake Run Rank 13 · compounded

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: coaster lift, drop, loop, and braking follow Newtonian mechanics and are engineered on every coaster.

A 500 kilogram roller coaster car: the chain lifts it, it drops, it loops, and the brakes bring it to a stop.

A roller coaster ride is a sequence because the track force changes from pushing up to pulling down and back again. A 500 kilogram car weighs 4905 newtons. On the lift hill the chain pulls up with 6000 newtons against gravity and 200 newtons of friction, so the net force is 895 newtons up and the car climbs slowly. On the drop gravity pulls down with 4905 newtons plus 95 newtons of drag, so the net force is 5000 newtons down and the car plunges. At the top of the loop the track pushes down with 3000 newtons on top of gravity's 4905, so the net force is 7905 newtons down, the centripetal force the loop requires. On the brake run the brakes push backward with 2000 newtons while the track holds the weight, and the car slows to a stop.

History

The first roller coasters were the ice slides of seventeenth-century Russia, and the first looping coaster, the Flip Flap Railway, opened at Coney Island in 1895. The modern steel coaster, with its lift hill, drop, loop, and brake run, was worked out in the twentieth century, and the loop only works because the car is moving fast enough for the track to hold it against the curve.

Method

Weigh the car and compute its weight from mass times gravity.; Take the chain pull and subtract gravity and friction for the lift step.; Add gravity and drag for the drop step.; Add the track force and gravity at the loop top and compare with the centripetal requirement.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Lift hill — forces:
    • Chain pull: 6000 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 4905 N at 270 degrees
    • Friction: 200 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 895 N; actual net force Fx = -5.70379e-13 N, Fy = 895 N; difference 5.70379e-13 N (tolerance 1 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Drop — forces:
    • Gravity: 4905 N at 270 degrees
    • Air drag: 95 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = -5000 N; actual net force Fx = -9.18485e-13 N, Fy = -5000 N; difference 9.18485e-13 N (tolerance 1 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Loop — forces:
    • Track normal force: 3000 N at 270 degrees
    • Gravity: 4905 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = -7905 N; actual net force Fx = -1.45212e-12 N, Fy = -7905 N; difference 1.45212e-12 N (tolerance 1 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Brake run — forces:
    • Braking force: 2000 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 4905 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 4905 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = -2000 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -2000 N, Fy = 2.44929e-13 N; difference 7.24762e-13 N (tolerance 1 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Run the steps in order: lift hill, drop, loop, brake run. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: the car must enter the loop fast enough for the track force to supply the centripetal requirement. Too slow, and the car cannot stay on the track.

Step 1, lift hill: chain pull 6000 newtons up against gravity 4905 newtons and friction 200 newtons down gives a net force of 895 newtons up. Step 2, drop: gravity 4905 newtons plus air drag 95 newtons down gives 5000 newtons down. Step 3, loop: track normal force 3000 newtons down plus gravity 4905 newtons down gives 7905 newtons down, the centripetal force the loop requires. Step 4, brake run: braking force 2000 newtons backward gives 2000 newtons backward while the track holds the weight.

Worked example

A 500 kilogram coaster car clicks slowly up the lift hill, the chain pulling it to the top. It tips over the crest and plunges, gaining speed all the way down, then races into the loop, where the track presses the riders into their seats at the top. It sweeps out of the loop, the brakes catch it on the final run, and it rolls gently back into the station.

Check by hand: 6000 minus 4905 minus 200 is 895 for the lift step, and 4905 plus 95 is 5000 for the drop step. The loop step is 3000 plus 4905, which is 7905, matching the stated net force.

Anti-Lock Braking: Apply, Slip, Release, Reapply, Stop Rank 14 · compounded

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: anti-lock braking cycles and the grip difference between rolling and sliding tyres are settled and measured.

A 1000 kilogram car braking hard: the brakes apply, a wheel slips, the system releases and reapplies, and the car stops under control.

Anti-lock braking is a sequence because the braking force changes every time a wheel slips. A 1000 kilogram car weighs 9810 newtons, held up by the same normal force. When the brakes apply, they push backward with 6000 newtons, so the net force is 6000 newtons backward and the car slows hard. If a wheel locks, the tyre slides and the grip falls to 3000 newtons backward, so the car slows half as fast and the wheel skids. The system releases the brake, the net force is zero for an instant, and the wheel spins up again. Then the brakes reapply with 6000 newtons, and the car stops under control instead of skidding.

History

Anti-lock braking was first developed for aircraft in the 1950s, where a skidding wheel on landing was dangerous, and reached cars in the 1970s, becoming standard in the 1990s and then legally required on new cars in many countries. The apply-slip-release-reapply cycle is the heart of the system: it keeps the tyres rolling, because a rolling tyre grips far harder than a sliding one.

Method

Weigh the car and compute the weight and the normal force.; Take the braking force at each step.; Add the horizontal forces at each step to get the net force.; Note the slip step, where the grip falls and the net force halves.; Run the demo, which checks all five steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Apply — forces:
    • Braking force: 6000 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 9810 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 9810 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = -6000 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -6000 N, Fy = 7.34788e-13 N; difference 1.16923e-12 N (tolerance 1 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Slip — forces:
    • Sliding friction: 3000 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 9810 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 9810 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = -3000 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -3000 N, Fy = 3.67394e-13 N; difference 1.41285e-12 N (tolerance 1 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Release — forces:
    • Normal force: 9810 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 9810 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.20138e-12 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.20138e-12 N (tolerance 1 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Reapply — forces:
    • Braking force: 6000 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 9810 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 9810 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = -6000 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -6000 N, Fy = 7.34788e-13 N; difference 1.16923e-12 N (tolerance 1 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  5. Step 5: Stop — forces:
    • Normal force: 9810 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 9810 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -1.20138e-12 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 1.20138e-12 N (tolerance 1 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: apply, slip, release, reapply, stop. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: the release must be fast enough to restore rolling before the skid grows. A slow release lets the wheel stay locked and the car skid.

Physics basis

Step 1, apply: braking force 6000 newtons backward gives a net force of 6000 newtons backward, which slows the car hard. Step 2, slip: sliding friction 3000 newtons backward alone gives 3000 newtons backward, so the car slows half as fast and the wheel skids. Step 3, release: no horizontal forces act, so the net force is zero and the wheel spins up. Step 4, reapply: braking force 6000 newtons backward again gives 6000 newtons backward. Step 5, stop: no horizontal forces act, so the net force is zero and the car stands still.

Worked example

A driver slams the brakes on a wet road. The car slows hard, then a wheel locks and the car starts to skid, so the anti-lock system releases that brake for a fraction of a second, the wheel spins up, and the brake reapplies. The cycle repeats many times a second, the car stays steerable, and it stops in a shorter distance than it would have skidding.

Evidence

established — Established textbook physics: anti-lock braking cycles and the grip difference between rolling and sliding tyres are settled and measured.

Verification path

Check by hand: the apply and reapply steps are the braking force alone, 6000 newtons backward, while the slip step is the sliding friction alone, 3000 newtons backward. The release and stop steps have no horizontal forces, so their net force is zero.

Science proof

In plain words, a rolling tyre grips the road and a sliding tyre does not, so the moment a wheel locks the brakes stop working well. The anti-lock system watches for that moment, lets go just long enough for the wheel to roll again, and then brakes again, repeating the cycle many times a second. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 5 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic, step by step: Step 'Apply': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -6000 N and Fy = 7.34788e-13 N, against a required net force of Fx = -6000 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.16923e-12 N against a tolerance of 1 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Slip': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -3000 N and Fy = 3.67394e-13 N, against a required net force of Fx = -3000 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.41285e-12 N against a tolerance of 1 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Release': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.20138e-12 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.20138e-12 N against a tolerance of 1 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Reapply': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -6000 N and Fy = 7.34788e-13 N, against a required net force of Fx = -6000 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.16923e-12 N against a tolerance of 1 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Stop': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.20138e-12 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 1.20138e-12 N against a tolerance of 1 N, so the step HOLDS. Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Anti-Lock Braking: Apply, Slip, Release, Reapply, Stop'
steps = [('Apply', [('Braking force', 6000.0, 180), ('Normal force', 9810.0, 90), ('Gravity', 9810.0, 270)], [-6000.0, 0.0], 1.0), ('Slip', [('Sliding friction', 3000.0, 180), ('Normal force', 9810.0, 90), ('Gravity', 9810.0, 270)], [-3000.0, 0.0], 1.0), ('Release', [('Normal force', 9810.0, 90), ('Gravity', 9810.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 1.0), ('Reapply', [('Braking force', 6000.0, 180), ('Normal force', 9810.0, 90), ('Gravity', 9810.0, 270)], [-6000.0, 0.0], 1.0), ('Stop', [('Normal force', 9810.0, 90), ('Gravity', 9810.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 1.0)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Crane Lift: Hoist, Slew, Lower, Set Down Rank 15 · compounded

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: crane cable tension and load handling are settled and calculated for every lift.

A tower crane lifting a 2000 kilogram load: the cable hoists it, the crane slews it sideways, lowers it, and sets it down.

A crane lift is a sequence because the cable tension changes at every step while the load moves through the air. A 2000 kilogram load weighs 19,620 newtons. To hoist, the cable pulls up with 21,000 newtons, so the net force is 1380 newtons up and the load rises. To slew, the cable pulls with 19,620 newtons, exactly matching the weight, while a 500 newton sideways push moves the load across, so the net force is 500 newtons sideways. To lower, the cable pulls with only 18,000 newtons, so the net force is 1620 newtons down and the load descends. At the ground the cable goes slack, the ground pushes up with 19,620 newtons, and the load rests.

History

Cranes have lifted loads since antiquity, from the treadwheel cranes of Roman building sites to the steam cranes of the nineteenth century and the tower cranes that build modern cities. The hoist-slew-lower-set-down sequence is the standard lift plan taught to every crane operator, and the cable tension at each step is the number the operator watches.

Method

Weigh the load and compute its weight from mass times gravity.; Set the cable tension for the hoist step above the weight.; Set the cable tension equal to the weight for the slew step and add the sideways push.; Set the cable tension below the weight for the lower step.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Hoist — forces:
    • Cable tension: 21000 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 19620 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 1380 N; actual net force Fx = -2.31826e-12 N, Fy = 1380 N; difference 2.31826e-12 N (tolerance 1 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Slew — forces:
    • Cable tension: 19620 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 19620 N at 270 degrees
    • Slew push: 500 N at 0 degrees
    required net force Fx = 500 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = 500 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 2.38742e-12 N (tolerance 1 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Lower — forces:
    • Cable tension: 18000 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 19620 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = -1620 N; actual net force Fx = -2.50195e-12 N, Fy = -1620 N; difference 2.50195e-12 N (tolerance 1 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Set down — forces:
    • Ground normal force: 19620 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 19620 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -2.40276e-12 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 2.40276e-12 N (tolerance 1 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: hoist, slew, lower, set down. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical value: cable tension 21,000 newtons in the hoist step. The cable and the crane must be rated above the tension the lift demands.

Physics basis

Step 1, hoist: cable tension 21,000 newtons up against gravity 19,620 newtons down gives a net force of 1380 newtons up. Step 2, slew: cable tension 19,620 newtons up against gravity 19,620 newtons down gives zero vertical net force, and the slew push of 500 newtons sideways gives a net force of 500 newtons sideways. Step 3, lower: cable tension 18,000 newtons up against gravity 19,620 newtons down gives 1620 newtons down. Step 4, set down: ground push 19,620 newtons up against gravity 19,620 newtons down gives zero net force.

Worked example

A tower crane picks up a 2000 kilogram steel beam from a truck. The cable tightens, the beam rises clear of the truck, and the crane slews it across the site while it hangs steady. The operator lowers it gently onto the frame, the cable goes slack as the beam's weight transfers to the structure, and the riggers unhook it.

Evidence

established — Established textbook physics: crane cable tension and load handling are settled and calculated for every lift.

Verification path

Check by hand: 2000 times 9.81 is 19,620, 21,000 minus 19,620 is 1380 for the hoist step, and 18,000 minus 19,620 is minus 1620 for the lower step. The slew step is an exact vertical balance, 19,620 against 19,620, with a 500 newton sideways net force.

Science proof

In plain words, a crane does not lift by pulling as hard as possible; it lifts by pulling a little harder than the weight while the load rises, exactly as hard as the weight while it hangs, and a little less hard while it lowers. The difference between the cable pull and the weight is the net force, and the net force is what moves the load. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic, step by step: Step 'Hoist': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -2.31826e-12 N and Fy = 1380 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 1380 N; the difference is 2.31826e-12 N against a tolerance of 1 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Slew': adding its member vectors gives Fx = 500 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 500 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 2.38742e-12 N against a tolerance of 1 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Lower': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -2.50195e-12 N and Fy = -1620 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = -1620 N; the difference is 2.50195e-12 N against a tolerance of 1 N, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Crane Lift: Hoist, Slew, Lower, Set Down'
steps = [('Hoist', [('Cable tension', 21000.0, 90), ('Gravity', 19620.0, 270)], [0.0, 1380.0], 1.0), ('Slew', [('Cable tension', 19620.0, 90), ('Gravity', 19620.0, 270), ('Slew push', 500.0, 0)], [500.0, 0.0], 1.0), ('Lower', [('Cable tension', 18000.0, 90), ('Gravity', 19620.0, 270)], [0.0, -1620.0], 1.0), ('Set down', [('Ground normal force', 19620.0, 90), ('Gravity', 19620.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 1.0)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Diving Board: Jump, Board Spring, Flight, Water Entry Rank 16 · compounded

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: springboard energy return and water-entry drag are settled and measured in diving.

A 70 kilogram diver on a springboard: the legs push, the board springs, the diver flies, and the water catches the entry.

A diving-board jump is a sequence because the upward force changes hands from legs to board to nothing to water. A 70 kilogram diver weighs 686.7 newtons. The legs push up with 1200 newtons, so the net force is 513.3 newtons up and the diver leaves the board rising. The board springs back with 1500 newtons up, so the net force is 813.3 newtons up and the diver is thrown higher. In flight gravity pulls down with 686.7 newtons plus 13.3 newtons of drag, so the net force is 700 newtons down and the diver arcs toward the water. On entry the water pushes up with 2000 newtons against the weight, so the net force is 1313.3 newtons up and the diver slows underwater.

History

Springboard diving became an Olympic sport in 1904 for men and 1920 for women, and the physics of the board has been studied ever since: the board is a spring that stores the diver's leg energy and returns it, throwing the diver higher than a jump from solid ground could. The jump-board-flight-entry sequence is the dive as the judges see it.

Method

Weigh the diver and compute the weight from mass times gravity.; Take the leg push and subtract the weight for the jump step.; Take the board force and subtract the weight for the board-spring step.; Add gravity and drag for the flight step and water drag for the entry step.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Jump — forces:
    • Leg push: 1200 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 686.7 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 513.3 N; actual net force Fx = -5.26659e-14 N, Fy = 513.3 N; difference 5.26659e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Board spring — forces:
    • Board force: 1500 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 686.7 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 813.3 N; actual net force Fx = -3.42962e-14 N, Fy = 813.3 N; difference 3.42962e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Flight — forces:
    • Gravity: 686.7 N at 270 degrees
    • Air drag: 13.3 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = -700 N; actual net force Fx = -1.28588e-13 N, Fy = -700 N; difference 1.28588e-13 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Water entry — forces:
    • Water drag: 2000 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 686.7 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 1313.3 N; actual net force Fx = -3.68006e-15 N, Fy = 1313.3 N; difference 3.68006e-15 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: jump, board spring, flight, water entry. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: the board must return enough force to clear the pool edge, and the water must be deep enough to absorb the entry. Either shortfall breaks the sequence.

Physics basis

Step 1, jump: leg push 1200 newtons up against gravity 686.7 newtons down gives a net force of 513.3 newtons up. Step 2, board spring: board force 1500 newtons up against gravity 686.7 newtons down gives 813.3 newtons up. Step 3, flight: gravity 686.7 newtons plus air drag 13.3 newtons down gives 700 newtons down. Step 4, water entry: water drag 2000 newtons up against gravity 686.7 newtons down gives 1313.3 newtons up, which slows the diver underwater.

Worked example

A 70 kilogram diver bounces on the end of a springboard, the board bending down and springing back. The legs push, the board throws the diver upward, and the diver rises, turns, and falls in a clean arc toward the pool. The water catches the entry with a rush of drag, the diver slows underwater, and surfaces to applause.

established — Established textbook physics: springboard energy return and water-entry drag are settled and measured in diving.

Verification path

Check by hand: 70 times 9.81 is 686.7, 1200 minus 686.7 is 513.3 for the jump step, and 1500 minus 686.7 is 813.3 for the board-spring step. The flight step is 686.7 plus 13.3, which is 700, and the entry step is 2000 minus 686.7, which is 1313.3.

Science proof

In plain words, the board is a spring that the diver loads with a jump and that pays the energy back with interest, throwing the diver higher than a jump from the ground. Once the diver leaves the board, gravity takes over and the flight is a simple arc, until the water pushes back harder than gravity pulls and the diver slows. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Diving Board: Jump, Board Spring, Flight, Water Entry'
steps = [('Jump', [('Leg push', 1200.0, 90), ('Gravity', 686.7, 270)], [0.0, 513.3], 0.5), ('Board spring', [('Board force', 1500.0, 90), ('Gravity', 686.7, 270)], [0.0, 813.3], 0.5), ('Flight', [('Gravity', 686.7, 270), ('Air drag', 13.3, 270)], [0.0, -700.0], 0.5), ('Water entry', [('Water drag', 2000.0, 90), ('Gravity', 686.7, 270)], [0.0, 1313.3], 0.5)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Spacecraft Rendezvous: Transfer Burn, Coast, Circularization, Docking Contact Rank 17 · complex

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: rendezvous profiles are settled and flown to the space station on every cargo and crew mission.

Two spacecraft meeting in orbit: a transfer burn, a long coast, a circularization burn, and a gentle docking contact, stated in model units.

A rendezvous is a sequence because the thrust is on for only two short steps and off for the long coast between them. In model units, the transfer burn pushes with 100 units forward against 80 units of gravity downward, so the net force is 100 units forward and 80 units down, lifting the chaser onto a higher path. On the coast, gravity alone pulls with 80 units down and the chaser drifts toward the target. The circularization burn pushes up with 80 units against gravity's 80 units down, so the net force is zero and the chaser matches the target's orbit. At docking contact, a 5 unit push meets a 5 unit thruster braking force, so the net force is zero and the two craft touch gently.

History

The first space rendezvous was flown by Gemini 6 and Gemini 7 in December 1965, and the first docking by Gemini 8 in March 1966. The transfer-coast-circularize-dock sequence has been the standard rendezvous profile ever since, flown by Apollo to the Moon, by the Space Shuttle to the Hubble telescope, and by every crew and cargo craft that visits the International Space Station.

Method

Take the model-unit thrust and gravity at each step.; Add thrust and gravity at each step to get the net force.; Check that the coast step has gravity alone as its net force.; Confirm the docking step has zero net force at contact.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Transfer burn — forces:
    • Thrust: 100 model units at 0 degrees
    • Gravity: 80 model units at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 100 model units, Fy = -80 model units; actual net force Fx = 100 model units, Fy = -80 model units; difference 1.42109e-14 model units (tolerance 0.5 model units); step verdict HOLDS.

  3. Step 3: Circularization — forces:
    • Thrust: 80 model units at 90 degrees

  4. Step 4: Docking contact — forces:
    • Contact force: 5 model units at 0 degrees
    • Thruster: 5 model units at 180 degrees

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: transfer burn, coast, circularization, docking contact. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: the transfer burn must place the chaser on a path that meets the target. A burn error grows during the coast and the rendezvous misses.

Physics basis

Step 1, transfer burn: thrust 100 model units forward against gravity 80 model units down gives a net force of 100 units forward and 80 units down. Step 2, coast: gravity 80 model units down alone gives a net force of 80 units down. Step 3, circularization: thrust 80 model units up against gravity 80 model units down gives zero net force. Step 4, docking contact: contact force 5 model units forward against thruster force 5 model units backward gives zero net force. Forces are stated in model units illustrating the balance; the established fact is the rendezvous profile itself, flown since Gemini in 1965.

Worked example

A cargo craft chases the space station around the Earth. It fires its engine for a transfer burn, coasts for hours while the gap closes, fires again to match the station's orbit, and then creeps in at centimetres per second until the docking mechanism catches with a soft clunk and the two craft become one.

Gravitational Slingshot: Approach, Periapsis Bend, Departure Rank 18 · complex

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: gravitational slingshots are settled and flown by Voyager, Cassini, and every outer-planet mission.

A spacecraft using a planet's gravity to gain speed: approach, the close bend around the planet, and departure, stated in model units.

A gravitational slingshot is a sequence because the planet's gravity does different work at each step. In model units, on approach the planet pulls the craft down with 100 units, speeding it up as it falls toward the planet. At periapsis, the closest point, gravity pulls with 150 units down while the curved path supplies a centrifugal effect of 100 units up, so the net force is 50 units down and the craft's direction bends sharply around the planet. On departure gravity pulls with only 60 units down as the craft climbs away, and the craft leaves faster than it arrived, having borrowed speed from the planet's motion around the Sun.

History

The slingshot was first proposed by Yuri Kondratyuk in 1918 and worked out by Friedrich Zander in the 1920s, and it was first used by Mariner 10 at Venus in 1974. Voyager 1 and Voyager 2 used slingshots at Jupiter and Saturn to reach the outer planets, and Cassini used four of them, at Venus, Earth, and Jupiter, to reach Saturn.

Method

Take the model-unit gravity at each step.; Add gravity and the centrifugal effect at the periapsis step.; Check that the approach step has gravity alone as its net force.; Confirm the departure step has gravity alone, weaker with distance.; Run the demo, which checks all three steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Approach — forces:
    • Gravity: 100 model units at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 model units, Fy = -100 model units; actual net force Fx = -1.83697e-14 model units, Fy = -100 model units; difference 1.83697e-14 model units (tolerance 0.5 model units); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Periapsis bend — forces:
    • Gravity: 150 model units at 270 degrees
    • Centrifugal effect: 100 model units at 90 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 model units, Fy = -50 model units; actual net force Fx = -2.14313e-14 model units, Fy = -50 model units; difference 2.14313e-14 model units (tolerance 0.5 model units); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Departure — forces:
    • Gravity: 60 model units at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 model units, Fy = -60 model units; actual net force Fx = -1.10218e-14 model units, Fy = -60 model units; difference 1.10218e-14 model units (tolerance 0.5 model units); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: approach, periapsis bend, departure. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: the aim at approach must place the craft at the planned periapsis distance. Too close and the craft enters the atmosphere; too far and the bend is too small.

Physics basis

Step 1, approach: gravity 100 model units down alone gives a net force of 100 units down, speeding the craft as it falls toward the planet. Step 2, periapsis bend: gravity 150 model units down against a centrifugal effect of 100 model units up gives 50 units down, which bends the path around the planet. Step 3, departure: gravity 60 model units down alone gives a net force of 60 units down as the craft climbs away, weaker with distance. Forces are stated in model units illustrating the balance; the established fact is the slingshot itself, flown by Voyager and Cassini.

Worked example

Voyager 2 falls toward Jupiter, gaining speed as the giant planet pulls it in. It whips around the planet at closest approach, its path bent by gravity, and climbs away again, leaving faster than it arrived and aimed onward to Saturn, Uranus, and Neptune, all without burning extra fuel. The same trick carried Cassini to Saturn on four flybys, and it is still the cheapest way to cross the Solar System.

Evidence

established — Established textbook physics: gravitational slingshots are settled and flown by Voyager, Cassini, and every outer-planet mission.

Verification path

Check by hand in model units: the approach step is gravity alone at 100 down, the periapsis step is 150 down minus 100 up, which is 50 down, and the departure step is gravity alone at 60 down, weaker than on approach because the craft is further away.

Science proof

In plain words, a planet is a moving wall: a spacecraft that falls toward it and climbs away again leaves with some of the planet's speed added to its own, like a ball bouncing off a moving train. The bend at closest approach is what turns the fall into a new direction. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 3 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Step 'Departure': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -1.10218e-14 model units and Fy = -60 model units, against a required net force of Fx = 0 model units and Fy = -60 model units; the difference is 1.10218e-14 model units against a tolerance of 0.5 model units, so the step HOLDS. The whole sequence therefore holds at every step. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Gravitational Slingshot: Approach, Periapsis Bend, Departure'
steps = [('Approach', [('Gravity', 100.0, 270)], [0.0, -100.0], 0.5), ('Periapsis bend', [('Gravity', 150.0, 270), ('Centrifugal effect', 100.0, 90)], [0.0, -50.0], 0.5), ('Departure', [('Gravity', 60.0, 270)], [0.0, -60.0], 0.5)]
unit = 'model units'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: forces are stated in model units illustrating the balance, not laboratory force readings; the established fact is stated in the record.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Earthquake Base Isolation: Ground Shake, Isolator Flex, Damper Absorb, Settle Rank 19 · complex

Evidence: established Established engineering physics: base isolation and damping are settled and built into hospitals and museums in earthquake country.

A base-isolated building in an earthquake: the ground shakes, the isolators flex, the dampers absorb the motion, and the building settles, stated in model units.

Base isolation is a sequence because the forces move from the ground to the isolators to the dampers in turn. In model units, when the ground shakes, it drives the building with 500 units sideways while the isolators push back with 400 units, so the net force is 100 units sideways and the building moves gently instead of snapping with the ground. As the isolators flex they push back with the full 500 units, so the net force is zero and the building rides out the shake. The dampers then absorb the motion, pushing back with 300 units against a 300 unit ground drive, so the net force is zero and the sway dies away. At settle the building's weight of 98,100 units down is held by the foundation's 98,100 units up, and the building stands still.

History

Base isolation was first proposed in the early twentieth century and first used on real buildings in the 1970s, with the first modern isolated buildings in New Zealand and Japan. The technique is now standard for hospitals, museums, and emergency centres in earthquake country: the building sits on flexible bearings that let the ground move beneath it while the building itself barely moves.

Method

Take the model-unit ground drive and isolator force at each step.; Add ground drive and isolator force at each step to get the net force.; Check that the isolator-flex step has zero net force.; Confirm the settle step has the building weight matched by the foundation.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Ground shake — forces:
    • Ground drive: 500 model units at 0 degrees
    • Isolator force: 400 model units at 180 degrees
    required net force Fx = 100 model units, Fy = 0 model units; actual net force Fx = 100 model units, Fy = 4.89859e-14 model units; difference 4.89859e-14 model units (tolerance 0.5 model units); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Isolator flex — forces:
    • Ground drive: 500 model units at 0 degrees
    • Isolator force: 500 model units at 180 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 model units, Fy = 0 model units; actual net force Fx = 0 model units, Fy = 6.12323e-14 model units; difference 6.12323e-14 model units (tolerance 0.5 model units); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Damper absorb — forces:
    • Ground drive: 300 model units at 0 degrees
    • Damper force: 300 model units at 180 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 model units, Fy = 0 model units; actual net force Fx = 0 model units, Fy = 3.67394e-14 model units; difference 3.67394e-14 model units (tolerance 0.5 model units); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Settle — forces:
    • Building weight: 98100 model units at 270 degrees
    • Foundation support: 98100 model units at 90 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 model units, Fy = 0 model units; actual net force Fx = -1.20138e-11 model units, Fy = 0 model units; difference 1.20138e-11 model units (tolerance 0.5 model units); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: ground shake, isolator flex, damper absorb, settle. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: the isolators must flex far enough to take the ground motion without reaching their travel limit. A quake bigger than the design limit breaks the sequence.

Physics basis

Step 1, ground shake: ground drive 500 model units sideways against isolator force 400 model units back gives a net force of 100 units sideways, so the building moves gently. Step 2, isolator flex: ground drive 500 model units against isolator force 500 model units gives zero net force. Step 3, damper absorb: ground drive 300 model units against damper force 300 model units gives zero net force, so the sway dies away. Step 4, settle: building weight 98,100 model units down against foundation support 98,100 model units up gives zero net force. Forces are stated in model units illustrating the balance; the established fact is base isolation itself, built into real hospitals.

Worked example

An earthquake shakes a hospital that sits on rubber-and-steel isolators. The ground jerks sideways, the isolators flex, and the building above sways gently instead of whipping with the ground. The dampers soak up the motion, the swaying shrinks with each cycle, and when the shaking stops the building is still standing on its foundations, its equipment undamaged and its wards open.

Sailboat Tack: Wind Drive, Turn, New Heading, Trim Rank 20 · complex

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: sail lift, keel resistance, and tacking are settled and sailed on every water.

A sailboat tacking into the wind: the wind drives it, it turns through the wind, settles on a new heading, and trims its sails.

A tack is a sequence because the wind force changes direction relative to the boat at every step. With the wind pushing 800 newtons forward, the keel resisting with 500 newtons backward, and water drag of 100 newtons down the line, the net force is 300 newtons forward and 100 newtons sideways, and the boat drives ahead. In the turn the wind pushes 800 newtons sideways, the keel resists with 500 newtons, and the rudder pushes 200 newtons backward, so the net force is 200 newtons backward and 300 newtons sideways, swinging the bow through the wind. On the new heading the forces are the same as before the turn, 300 newtons forward and 100 newtons sideways. Trimmed, the wind is 500 newtons, the keel 400, and the drag 100, so the net force is 100 newtons forward and 100 newtons sideways, and the boat holds its course.

History

Sailing into the wind by tacking, zigzagging with the wind first on one side and then on the other, has been the heart of sailing since the lateen sail spread around the Mediterranean in the Middle Ages. The physics is the same as an aircraft wing: the sail is a wing that pulls the boat forward even when the wind is ahead of it, and the keel stops the boat from sliding sideways.

Method

Take the wind force, the keel resistance, and the water drag at each step.; Add the forces as vectors at each step to get the net force.; Check that the turn step's net force points the bow through the wind.; Confirm the trim step has a small steady forward net force.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Wind drive — forces:
    • Wind force: 800 N at 0 degrees
    • Keel resistance: 500 N at 180 degrees
    • Water drag: 100 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 300 N, Fy = -100 N; actual net force Fx = 300 N, Fy = -100 N; difference 5.68434e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Turn — forces:
    • Wind force: 800 N at 90 degrees
    • Keel resistance: 500 N at 270 degrees
    • Rudder force: 200 N at 180 degrees
    required net force Fx = -200 N, Fy = 300 N; actual net force Fx = -200 N, Fy = 300 N; difference 5.68434e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: New heading — forces:
    • Wind force: 800 N at 0 degrees
    • Keel resistance: 500 N at 180 degrees
    • Water drag: 100 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 300 N, Fy = -100 N; actual net force Fx = 300 N, Fy = -100 N; difference 5.68434e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Trim — forces:
    • Wind force: 500 N at 0 degrees
    • Keel resistance: 400 N at 180 degrees
    • Water drag: 100 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 100 N, Fy = -100 N; actual net force Fx = 100 N, Fy = -100 N; difference 4.49387e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: wind drive, turn, new heading, trim. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: the boat must keep enough speed through the turn to steer. Too slow, and it stalls head-to-wind and the tack fails.

Physics basis

Step 1, wind drive: wind force 800 newtons forward against keel resistance 500 newtons backward and water drag 100 newtons sideways gives a net force of 300 newtons forward and 100 newtons sideways. Step 2, turn: wind force 800 newtons sideways against keel resistance 500 newtons and rudder force 200 newtons backward gives 200 newtons backward and 300 newtons sideways, swinging the bow through the wind. Step 3, new heading: the same forces as step 1 give 300 newtons forward and 100 newtons sideways. Step 4, trim: wind force 500 newtons against keel resistance 400 newtons and drag 100 newtons gives 100 newtons forward and 100 newtons sideways.

Worked example

A sailboat beats upwind toward a mark. It sails close-hauled with the wind on one side, the crew calls the tack, the helm goes over, and the bow swings through the wind while the sails flap and fill on the other side. The crew trims the sheets, the boat settles on its new heading, and the zigzag continues toward the mark.

Evidence

established — Established textbook physics: sail lift, keel resistance, and tacking are settled and sailed on every water.

Verification path

Check by hand: 800 minus 500 is 300 forward and the drag is 100 sideways for the wind-drive step, and 800 minus 500 is 300 sideways with the rudder's 200 backward for the turn step. The trim step is 500 minus 400, which is 100 forward.

A tack is the boat turning through the wind so the wing can pull from the other side. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Sailboat Tack: Wind Drive, Turn, New Heading, Trim'
steps = [('Wind drive', [('Wind force', 800.0, 0), ('Keel resistance', 500.0, 180), ('Water drag', 100.0, 270)], [300.0, -100.0], 0.5), ('Turn', [('Wind force', 800.0, 90), ('Keel resistance', 500.0, 270), ('Rudder force', 200.0, 180)], [-200.0, 300.0], 0.5), ('New heading', [('Wind force', 800.0, 0), ('Keel resistance', 500.0, 180), ('Water drag', 100.0, 270)], [300.0, -100.0], 0.5), ('Trim', [('Wind force', 500.0, 0), ('Keel resistance', 400.0, 180), ('Water drag', 100.0, 270)], [100.0, -100.0], 0.5)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")
