Gymnast Vault: Run, Springboard, Flight, Landing Rank 21 · complex

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: springboard launch and gymnastic flight follow Newtonian mechanics and are measured in every competition.

A 60 kilogram gymnast vaulting: the run-up, the springboard, the flight over the vault, and the landing.

A vault is a sequence because the upward force changes hands from legs to board to nothing to mat. A 60 kilogram gymnast weighs 588.6 newtons. On the run the legs drive forward with 700 newtons against 100 newtons of friction, so the net force is 600 newtons forward and the gymnast builds speed. On the springboard the board pushes up with 1500 newtons against the weight, so the net force is 911.4 newtons up and the gymnast is launched. In flight gravity alone pulls down with 588.6 newtons, so the net force is 588.6 newtons down and the gymnast arcs over the vault. On landing the mat pushes up with 1200 newtons against the weight, so the net force is 611.4 newtons up and the gymnast is brought to a stop.

History

Vaulting has been a gymnastics event since the first modern Olympics in 1896, and the springboard, introduced in the twentieth century, changed it from a jump into a launch. The run-springboard-flight-landing sequence is the vault as the judges score it, and the physics is the same as any spring launch: speed in, height out.

Method

Weigh the gymnast and compute the weight from mass times gravity.; Take the leg drive and subtract friction for the run step.; Take the board force and subtract the weight for the springboard step.; Take gravity alone for the flight step and the mat force for the landing step.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Run — forces:
    • Leg drive: 700 N at 0 degrees
    • Friction: 100 N at 180 degrees
    • Normal force: 588.6 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 588.6 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 600 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = 600 N, Fy = 1.22465e-14 N; difference 1.14345e-13 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Springboard — forces:
    • Board force: 1500 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 588.6 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 911.4 N; actual net force Fx = -1.62756e-14 N, Fy = 911.4 N; difference 1.62756e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Flight — forces:
    • Gravity: 588.6 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = -588.6 N; actual net force Fx = -1.08124e-13 N, Fy = -588.6 N; difference 1.08124e-13 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Landing — forces:
    • Mat force: 1200 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 588.6 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 611.4 N; actual net force Fx = -3.46453e-14 N, Fy = 611.4 N; difference 3.46453e-14 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: run, springboard, flight, landing. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

stable — Critical condition: the run must build enough speed for the board to launch the gymnast over the vault. Too slow, and the gymnast never clears it.

Physics basis

Step 1, run: leg drive 700 newtons forward against friction 100 newtons backward gives a net force of 600 newtons forward. Step 2, springboard: board force 1500 newtons up against gravity 588.6 newtons down gives 911.4 newtons up. Step 3, flight: gravity 588.6 newtons down alone gives a net force of 588.6 newtons down. Step 4, landing: mat force 1200 newtons up against gravity 588.6 newtons down gives 611.4 newtons up, which stops the gymnast.

Worked example

A 60 kilogram gymnast sprints down the runway, hits the springboard with both feet, and is thrown upward, twisting over the vault in a clean arc. The hands touch the vault, the body straightens, and the gymnast comes down feet first on the mat, the mat giving under the landing and bringing the gymnast to a stop, arms raised.

Evidence

Verification path

Check by hand: 60 times 9.81 is 588.6, 700 minus 100 is 600 for the run step, and 1500 minus 588.6 is 911.4 for the springboard step. The flight step is gravity alone at 588.6 down, and the landing step is 1200 minus 588.6, which is 611.4.

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Gymnast Vault: Run, Springboard, Flight, Landing'
steps = [('Run', [('Leg drive', 700.0, 0), ('Friction', 100.0, 180), ('Normal force', 588.6, 90), ('Gravity', 588.6, 270)], [600.0, 0.0], 0.5), ('Springboard', [('Board force', 1500.0, 90), ('Gravity', 588.6, 270)], [0.0, 911.4], 0.5), ('Flight', [('Gravity', 588.6, 270)], [0.0, -588.6], 0.5), ('Landing', [('Mat force', 1200.0, 90), ('Gravity', 588.6, 270)], [0.0, 611.4], 0.5)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")

Particle Accelerator: Injection, Radio-Frequency Acceleration, Magnetic Bending, Extraction Rank 22 · complex

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: accelerator injection, radio-frequency acceleration, magnetic bending, and extraction are settled and run daily.

A particle beam in an accelerator: injection, radio-frequency kicks, magnetic bending around the ring, and extraction, stated in model units.

An accelerator cycle is a sequence because a different force takes the lead at each step. In model units, injection kicks the beam with 50 units forward, so the net force is 50 units forward and the particles enter the ring. The radio-frequency cavities kick with 40 units forward against 5 units of drag, so the net force is 35 units forward and the beam gains energy on every lap. The bending magnets pull with 100 units down while the cavities add 10 units forward, so the net force is 10 units forward and 100 units down, bending the beam around the ring. At extraction a kicker pushes up with 80 units against the bending magnets' 80 units down, so the net force is zero and the beam leaves the ring cleanly toward its target experiment.

History

The first cyclotron was built by Ernest Lawrence in 1930, and the first synchrotron followed in the 1940s. The injection-acceleration-bending-extraction sequence is the cycle of every circular accelerator, from hospital proton-therapy machines to the Large Hadron Collider, which bends two beams around a 27 kilometre ring with superconducting magnets.

Method

Take the model-unit injection kick for the first step.; Add the radio-frequency kick and subtract drag for the acceleration step.; Add the magnetic force and the cavity kick for the bending step.; Balance the kicker against the magnets for the extraction step.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and prints the sequence verdict..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Injection — forces:
    • Injection kick: 50 model units at 0 degrees
    required net force Fx = 50 model units, Fy = 0 model units; actual net force Fx = 50 model units, Fy = 0 model units; difference 0 model units (tolerance 0.5 model units); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Radio-frequency acceleration — forces:
    • Radio-frequency kick: 40 model units at 0 degrees
    • Drag: 5 model units at 180 degrees
    required net force Fx = 35 model units, Fy = 0 model units; actual net force Fx = 35 model units, Fy = 6.12323e-16 model units; difference 6.12323e-16 model units (tolerance 0.5 model units); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Magnetic bending — forces:
    • Magnetic force: 100 model units at 270 degrees
    • Radio-frequency kick: 10 model units at 0 degrees
    required net force Fx = 10 model units, Fy = -100 model units; actual net force Fx = 10 model units, Fy = -100 model units; difference 1.77636e-14 model units (tolerance 0.5 model units); step verdict HOLDS.
  4. Step 4: Extraction — forces:
    • Kicker force: 80 model units at 90 degrees
    • Magnetic force: 80 model units at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 model units, Fy = 0 model units; actual net force Fx = -9.79717e-15 model units, Fy = 0 model units; difference 9.79717e-15 model units (tolerance 0.5 model units); step verdict HOLDS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: injection, radio-frequency acceleration, magnetic bending, extraction. The sequence holds when every step's actual net force equals the net force that step's motion requires.

Collapse verdict

conditionally stable — Critical condition: the bending magnets must supply the centripetal force for the beam's energy at every lap. A magnet failure lets the beam hit the wall.

Physics basis

Step 1, injection: injection kick 50 model units forward alone gives a net force of 50 units forward. Step 2, radio-frequency acceleration: radio-frequency kick 40 model units forward against drag 5 model units backward gives 35 units forward. Step 3, magnetic bending: magnetic force 100 model units down plus radio-frequency kick 10 model units forward gives a net force of 10 units forward and 100 units down, which bends the beam around the ring. Step 4, extraction: kicker force 80 model units up against magnetic force 80 model units down gives zero net force. Forces are stated in model units illustrating the balance; the established fact is the accelerator cycle itself, run daily in machines from hospitals to the Large Hadron Collider.

Worked example

In a hospital proton-therapy accelerator, protons are injected into the ring, kicked forward by radio-frequency cavities on every lap, and bent around the ring by magnets thousands of times a second. When they reach the treatment energy, a kicker magnet nudges them out of the ring and down a beamline to the patient, where they stop in the tumour and deliver their dose.

Evidence

established — Established textbook physics: accelerator injection, radio-frequency acceleration, magnetic bending, and extraction are settled and run daily.

Verification path

Check by hand in model units: the injection step is the kick alone at 50 forward, the acceleration step is 40 minus 5, which is 35, and the extraction step is 80 up against 80 down, which is zero. The bending step is 10 forward and 100 down.

Science proof

In plain words, an accelerator is a racetrack for particles: the cavities are the engine that speeds them up a little on every lap, the magnets are the steering that bends them around the ring, and the kicker is the exit gate that lets them out when they are fast enough. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: all 4 steps hold within tolerance, so the whole sequence holds.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Particle Accelerator: Injection, Radio-Frequency Acceleration, Magnetic Bending, Extraction'
steps = [('Injection', [('Injection kick', 50.0, 0)], [50.0, 0.0], 0.5), ('Radio-frequency acceleration', [('Radio-frequency kick', 40.0, 0), ('Drag', 5.0, 180)], [35.0, 0.0], 0.5), ('Magnetic bending', [('Magnetic force', 100.0, 270), ('Radio-frequency kick', 10.0, 0)], [10.0, -100.0], 0.5), ('Extraction', [('Kicker force', 80.0, 90), ('Magnetic force', 80.0, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.5)]
unit = 'model units'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: forces are stated in model units illustrating the balance, not laboratory force readings; the established fact is stated in the record.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")

Overloaded Crane Lift: Hoist, Overload, Cable Yield, Collapse Rank 23 · complex

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: cable yield and overload failure are settled, and crane safety ratings exist because of them.

A crane lift that goes wrong: the cable hoists a 2000 kilogram load, extra load is added past the rating, the cable yields, and the load falls.

This sequence collapses, and the demo shows exactly where. A 2000 kilogram load weighs 19,620 newtons. The hoist step is sound: the cable pulls up with 21,000 newtons, so the net force is 1380 newtons up and the load rises. Then extra load is added until the weight is 25,000 newtons, while the cable still pulls with only 21,000 newtons, so the net force is 4000 newtons down against a required net force of zero: the load cannot be held and starts to fall. The cable yields under the overload, its pull dropping to 15,000 newtons, so the net force is 10,000 newtons down. Finally the cable parts, the pull is zero, and the net force is the full 25,000 newtons down as the load falls.

History

Crane collapses are studied by every safety authority because the failure is always the same: a load past the rating, a cable past its yield, and then gravity. The safe working load of a crane is set well below the breaking load precisely so that the overload step in this record never happens on a real site, and load charts, limit switches, and rated-capacity indicators exist to stop it.

Method

Weigh the load and compute its weight from mass times gravity.; Check the hoist step, where the cable pull exceeds the weight.; Add the extra load and compare the new weight with the cable pull.; Note the yield step, where the cable pull falls as the cable stretches past its limit.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and shows the sequence failing..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Hoist — forces:
    • Cable tension: 21000 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 19620 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 1380 N; actual net force Fx = -2.31826e-12 N, Fy = 1380 N; difference 2.31826e-12 N (tolerance 1 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Overload — forces:
    • Cable tension: 21000 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 25000 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -3.30655e-12 N, Fy = -4000 N; difference 4000 N (tolerance 1 N); step verdict FAILS.
  3. Step 3: Cable yield — forces:
    • Cable tension: 15000 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 25000 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -3.67394e-12 N, Fy = -10000 N; difference 10000 N (tolerance 1 N); step verdict FAILS.
  4. Step 4: Collapse — forces:
    • Gravity: 25000 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -4.59243e-12 N, Fy = -25000 N; difference 25000 N (tolerance 1 N); step verdict FAILS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: hoist, overload, cable yield, collapse. The sequence collapses: the overload step fails because the weight exceeds the cable pull, and the later steps fail worse.

Collapse verdict

collapses — Critical value: safe working load 19,620 newtons against a cable pull of 21,000 newtons. Past the rating the cable cannot hold, and the sequence collapses.

Physics basis

Step 1, hoist: cable tension 21,000 newtons up against gravity 19,620 newtons down gives a net force of 1380 newtons up, which holds. Step 2, overload: cable tension 21,000 newtons up against gravity 25,000 newtons down gives 4000 newtons down against a required net force of zero, so the step fails and the load starts to fall. Step 3, cable yield: cable tension 15,000 newtons up against gravity 25,000 newtons down gives 10,000 newtons down, so the step fails. Step 4, collapse: gravity 25,000 newtons down alone gives 25,000 newtons down, so the load falls.

Worked example

A crane lifts a 2000 kilogram beam, and a second load is hooked on without checking the chart, taking the weight to 25,000 newtons. The cable strains, the load starts to sink instead of holding, and the cable yields, stretching and weakening until it parts. The load falls to the ground. The accident report records the same three numbers this demo records: the rated load, the actual load, and the cable that could not hold it.

Evidence

established — Established textbook physics: cable yield and overload failure are settled, and crane safety ratings exist because of them.

Verification path

Check by hand: 2000 times 9.81 is 19,620, and 21,000 minus 19,620 is 1380 for the hoist step. The overload step is 21,000 minus 25,000, which is minus 4000, and the yield step is 15,000 minus 25,000, which is minus 10,000, so both fail their required zero net force by far.

Generator

Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

Science proof

In plain words, a cable can only pull as hard as its steel allows, and a load heavier than that pull will fall no matter what the operator wants. The overload step is the moment the numbers stop adding up: the weight is bigger than the pull, so the net force points down and the load accelerates downward. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order, and the last three steps fail. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: at least one step fails its tolerance check, so the whole sequence fails.

Math proof

Step 'Overload': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -3.30655e-12 N and Fy = -4000 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 4000 N against a tolerance of 1 N, so the step FAILS. The whole sequence therefore fails. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Spring Over-Compression: Compress, Release, Over-Compress, Yield Rank 24 · complex

Evidence: established Established textbook physics: Hooke's law and the elastic limit are settled, and spring yield is directly measurable.

A 5 kilogram mass on a spring rated to 80 newtons: compressed inside its range, released, forced past its elastic limit, and permanently yielded.

This sequence collapses, and the demo shows exactly where. A 5 kilogram mass weighs 49.05 newtons on a spring of stiffness 100 newtons per metre, rated to an elastic limit of 80 newtons of spring force. Compressed inside the range, the hand pushes down with 30 newtons, the spring pushes up with 79.05 newtons, and gravity pulls down with 49.05 newtons, so the net force is zero. Released, the spring's 79.05 newtons up beats gravity's 49.05 newtons down, so the net force is 30 newtons up. Forced past the limit, the hand pushes down with 100 newtons while the spring can only supply its limit of 80 newtons up, so the net force is 69.05 newtons down against a required zero: the spring yields. After yielding the spring only supplies 40 newtons up against the weight, so the net force is 9.05 newtons down and the mass never returns to its old balance.

History

Hooke stated the spring law in 1678, and the elastic limit, the point past which a spring does not spring back, has been part of materials science ever since. Every spring is rated for a maximum force, and the yield in this record is the same failure the spring-past-its-limit force combination records: the material deforms permanently and the old balance is gone.

Method

Weigh the mass and compute its weight from mass times gravity.; Take the spring stiffness and the elastic limit of 80 newtons of spring force.; Add hand, spring, and gravity at the compress step and check the sum is zero.; Push past the limit and compare the hand force with the spring's maximum.; Run the demo, which checks all four steps in order and shows the sequence failing..

Sequence steps

  1. Step 1: Compress — forces:
    • Hand push: 30 N at 270 degrees
    • Spring force: 79.05 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 49.05 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -9.68083e-15 N, Fy = 0 N; difference 9.68083e-15 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  2. Step 2: Release — forces:
    • Spring force: 79.05 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 49.05 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 30 N; actual net force Fx = -4.16992e-15 N, Fy = 30 N; difference 4.16992e-15 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict HOLDS.
  3. Step 3: Over-compress — forces:
    • Hand push: 100 N at 270 degrees
    • Spring force: 80 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 49.05 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -2.24815e-14 N, Fy = -69.05 N; difference 69.05 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict FAILS.
  4. Step 4: Yield — forces:
    • Spring force: 40 N at 90 degrees
    • Gravity: 49.05 N at 270 degrees
    required net force Fx = 0 N, Fy = 0 N; actual net force Fx = -6.56105e-15 N, Fy = -9.05 N; difference 9.05 N (tolerance 0.5 N); step verdict FAILS.

Sequence rule

Run the steps in order: compress, release, over-compress, yield. The sequence collapses: the over-compress step fails because the hand exceeds the spring's elastic limit, and the yield step fails after it.

Collapse verdict

collapses — Critical value: elastic limit 80 newtons of spring force. Past it the spring yields permanently, and the sequence collapses.

Physics basis

Step 1, compress: spring force 79.05 newtons up against hand push 30 newtons down and gravity 49.05 newtons down gives zero net force. Step 2, release: spring force 79.05 newtons up against gravity 49.05 newtons down gives 30 newtons up. Step 3, over-compress: spring force 80 newtons up, its elastic limit, against hand push 100 newtons down and gravity 49.05 newtons down gives 69.05 newtons down against a required net force of zero, so the step fails and the spring yields. Step 4, yield: spring force 40 newtons up against gravity 49.05 newtons down gives 9.05 newtons down, so the step fails and the mass never returns to its old balance.

Worked example

A student compresses a spring with a 5 kilogram mass, holding it with a 30 newton push, and lets go: the mass bounces happily. Then the student leans on it with 100 newtons, past the spring's 80 newton limit. The spring gives with a soft click and stays bent. Afterwards the mass hangs lower than before, because the yielded spring only pushes with 40 newtons where it used to push with 49.05.

Evidence

established — Established textbook physics: Hooke's law and the elastic limit are settled, and spring yield is directly measurable.

Verification path

Check by hand: 5 times 9.81 is 49.05, and 79.05 minus 49.05 minus 30 is zero for the compress step, while 79.05 minus 49.05 is 30 for the release step. The over-compress step is 80 minus 49.05 minus 100, which is minus 69.05, failing its required zero.

Generator

Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

Science proof

In plain words, a spring is only a spring while it stays inside its elastic range; push it past the limit and the metal itself gives way, stretching permanently instead of pushing back. After that the spring is a different, weaker spring, and the old balance point is gone for good. The demo proves the arithmetic by checking each step in order, and the last two steps fail. The demo proves the sequence by checking each step in order: at least one step fails its tolerance check, so the whole sequence fails.

Math proof

Worked arithmetic, step by step: Step 'Compress': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -9.68083e-15 N and Fy = 0 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 9.68083e-15 N against a tolerance of 0.5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Release': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -4.16992e-15 N and Fy = 30 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 30 N; the difference is 4.16992e-15 N against a tolerance of 0.5 N, so the step HOLDS. Step 'Over-compress': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -2.24815e-14 N and Fy = -69.05 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 69.05 N against a tolerance of 0.5 N, so the step FAILS. Step 'Yield': adding its member vectors gives Fx = -6.56105e-15 N and Fy = -9.05 N, against a required net force of Fx = 0 N and Fy = 0 N; the difference is 9.05 N against a tolerance of 0.5 N, so the step FAILS. The whole sequence therefore fails. The demo runs every step twice, hashes each result with SHA-256, and prints MATCH, so a fresh run of the demo verifies this proof exactly.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import json
import math

title = 'Spring Over-Compression: Compress, Release, Over-Compress, Yield'
steps = [('Compress', [('Hand push', 30.0, 270), ('Spring force', 79.05, 90), ('Gravity', 49.05, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.5), ('Release', [('Spring force', 79.05, 90), ('Gravity', 49.05, 270)], [0.0, 30.0], 0.5), ('Over-compress', [('Hand push', 100.0, 270), ('Spring force', 80.0, 90), ('Gravity', 49.05, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.5), ('Yield', [('Spring force', 40.0, 90), ('Gravity', 49.05, 270)], [0.0, 0.0], 0.5)]
unit = 'N'

def net(forces):
    fx = sum(m * math.cos(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    fy = sum(m * math.sin(math.radians(a)) for _n, m, a in forces)
    return fx, fy

def run_once():
    results = []
    for name, forces, required, tolerance in steps:
        fx, fy = net(forces)
        diff = math.hypot(fx - required[0], fy - required[1])
        verdict = "HOLDS" if diff <= tolerance else "FAILS"
        results.append((name, fx, fy, diff, verdict))
    overall = "HOLDS" if all(v == "HOLDS" for _n, _x, _y, _d, v in results) else "FAILS"
    return results, overall

results, overall = run_once()
digest_a = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results] + [overall], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
results2, overall2 = run_once()
digest_b = hashlib.sha256(json.dumps([[round(fx, 6), round(fy, 6), round(diff, 6), v] for _n, fx, fy, diff, v in results2] + [overall2], sort_keys=True).encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Force sequence: " + title)
for i, (name, forces, required, tolerance) in enumerate(steps, 1):
    print("Step %d: %s" % (i, name))
    for fname, mag, ang in forces:
        print("  " + fname + ": " + ("%g" % mag) + " " + unit + " at " + ("%g" % ang) + " degrees")
    _n, fx, fy, diff, verdict = results[i - 1]
    print("  Net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s, |F| = %s %s" % (("%g" % fx), unit, ("%g" % fy), unit, ("%g" % math.hypot(fx, fy)), unit))
    print("  Required net force: Fx = %s %s, Fy = %s %s" % (("%g" % required[0]), unit, ("%g" % required[1]), unit))
    print("  Difference from required: %s %s (tolerance %s %s)" % (("%g" % diff), unit, ("%g" % tolerance), unit))
    print("  Step verdict: " + verdict)
print("Sequence verdict: " + overall + " - the ordered force sequence " + ("holds at every step." if overall == "HOLDS" else "fails: at least one step exceeds its equilibrium tolerance, so the sequence runs away or collapses."))
print("SHA-256 run 1: " + digest_a)
print("SHA-256 run 2: " + digest_b)
if digest_a == digest_b:
    print("Deterministic: MATCH - both runs produced the same force sequence.")
else:
    print("Deterministic: MISMATCH - the two runs disagreed.")
print("Note: this demo recomputes the force sequence from the record's own fixed inputs; it is arithmetic on the sequence, not a laboratory measurement.")
